Opinion: Onimusha vs Phantom Blade Zero - The Insane Sword Fight for Game of the Year

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Onimusha: Way of the Sword and Phantom Blade Zero are two of the best action games I've played in years.

I've always been a sucker for cool sword fights and I'd argue that these two games do sword fights better than almost any other (Ghost of Tsushima and Ghost of Yotei deserve a big shoutout, mind).

I genuinely feel like I've hit the jackpot, having both of these blade-wielding masterpieces drop within the space of two months.

And I've got very little doubt: it's going to be Onimusha and Phantom Blade Zero vying for my eventual Game of the Year vote. And at the time of writing this article, I've got no idea which way I'm going to swing.

To be clear, I know Grand Theft Auto 6 is just around the corner — and yes, it could absolutely throw a spanner in the works — but taking my own personal taste into account, it would have to be properly mind-blowing to top the two titles I've already mentioned.

So I wanted to try and take a step back, and re-examine both Onimusha and Phantom Blade Zero. Break them down a bit and at least attempt to decide on which one I like better.

Admittedly, we might not get to that point today, but it should still make for a good discussion on what actually makes a great action game.

Believe it or not, enjoyable action is crucial to making a top tier action title — but picking a winner in this category isn't going to be easy.

That's mainly because the two games operate so differently. While they share a focus on defensive techniques that subsequently open your opponents up to retaliation, the overall feel is completely different.

Onimusha is very measured and, for lack of a better word, slow. Comparatively, Phantom Blade Zero is lightning quick. Their rhythms couldn't be much more distinct from one another.

Interestingly, though, both games prioritise cinematic flair — but critically, they do it without sacrificing responsive controls. I think this is a huge factor in why they stand apart from many of their peers; both games are a masterclass in providing eye-popping but very readable action.

Although I will say, Phantom Blade Zero pushes the limits of that last point at times. Where Onimusha affords itself clarity through its more methodical clashing of swords, Zero can appear a little too busy during its more frantic battles.

And I think this translates, very subtly, into Phantom Blade Zero feeling more unwieldy. You press a button once in Onimusha and Musashi swings his sword once. You do the same in Zero and Soul might slash three times depending on the weapon he has equipped.

That's part of Zero's wuxia identity, of course, but the seemingly never-ending combos can sometimes come off as being flashy for the sake of flashiness. In contrast, every strike in Capcom's offering feels like it has a purpose and a weight to it.

So suddenly, I find myself leaning towards Onimusha here... but then Zero takes the cake with regards to variety.

I absolutely love the weapon system in S-GAME's debut. There are 24 of them to find throughout the game, and they're all unique, with their own movesets and accompanying perks. Coming across a new blade is genuinely exciting, because you know the developer's put serious time and effort into designing them.

Onimusha just can't compete — but that's also by design. Capcom clearly wanted to flesh out Musashi's single sword to an obsessive extent, and that's exactly what it did. He's got one primary weapon, and you learn to master it over the course of his adventure.

And this is reflected in the sheer number of unique, contextual animations found in Onimusha. You can't really delve into that level of detail if you're animating for 24 weapons — although S-GAME certainly tries, with each blade boasting its own finishers and standard combos.

It's a real 50-50, then; the action is absolutely brilliant in both games, but for different reasons. There's part of me, though, that just adores Capcom's commitment to weight. Whether it's Monster Hunter, Devil May Cry, Dragon's Dogma, or Onimusha, no one does satisfyingly crunchy combat like the Japanese company.

And so purely in terms of feel, I think Onimusha sneaks the win. It's a more refined experience overall.

Bosses play a gigantic role in this debate. They're the highlights of both games, punctuating pivotal story moments, providing sheer spectacle, and putting your skills to the test.

Numbers-wise, Phantom Blade Zero wins this one hands down. The game's got a really, really impressive lineup of unique boss battles; outside of several big bad guys that share select attack animations, there are no repeats to be found.

I honestly can't think of another action title that can equal its roster.

You could argue, however, that Onimusha's more focused approach makes each boss feel more memorable. I do think Zero's myriad opponents blend together a touch; I'd struggle to recall a lot of them if you were to put me on the spot.

But Onimusha also has a repeat problem. It reuses certain boss encounters multiple times, and even though the act of clashing with them is always engaging because of how strong the combat system is, it does get to a point later on where you find yourself asking "this guy again?".

The bottom line here is that both games have a range of incredible boss encounters — Onimusha in particular's got a few of my all-time favourites, like the brothers — but ultimately, it's so hard to look past the variety that's on display in Phantom Blade Zero.

When it comes to story and characters, I don't think there's any competition.

Phantom Blade Zero's narrative is arguably its weakest aspect. I think the plot itself is quite interesting once events start falling into place in the game's latter half, but until then, it's a mishmash of often incoherent flashbacks and exposition-heavy dialogue.

The story's sloppily told, and there are way too many notes and memory flowers — basically audio logs — to find in order to understand the full context of what's going on.

By contrast, Onimusha tells a story that's smaller in overall scope, with a greater emphasis on character interactions. The narrative stuff never outstays its welcome, with snappy dialogue and frankly fantastic cutscenes.

Plus, Musashi is a deeply endearing protagonist.

Phantom Blade Zero's got some amazing cinematics as well — the animated ones are superb — but they can't have the same impact when the context surrounding them is often so murky.

Placing great gameplay within a world that's lovely to look at and a joy to explore can be what truly elevates an action game.

The recent Ninja Gaiden 4, for example, is an absolute blast to play — but its drab, samey environments prevent it from feeling like a memorable journey.

Phantom Blade Zero certainly doesn't have that problem. Each chapter takes place within a visually distinct region. Most are very linear in structure, but when all the emphasis is on the action, that's a positive — all you really need is a nice backdrop.

Onimusha stumbles slightly in that sense. Between main story segments, you get to roam freely around a kind-of open world city map which gradually expands as you progress through the game.

The problem is it just gets a bit dreary; there are only so many times you can run down the same grey and brown streets before they start to feel like filler.

And while I think Phantom Blade Zero makes you backtrack a touch too often, the region-based structure promotes a sense of adventure and travel across a more fleshed out world.

Yep, as expected, I'm having a seriously tough time trying to separate these two titles. If anything, writing this article has just made me appreciate their individual strengths even more.

Onimusha is the action connoisseur's pick, I reckon. It's the refined choice, a more nuanced but masterful take on the genre from the veterans at Capcom.

Meanwhile, Phantom Blade Zero is the blockbuster. It's bigger in scope and ambition, and its action is consistently extreme. It's arguably the more exciting release, from a relatively new studio that could become a giant of the genre.

It's a good job I've got another month or so to think this one over...

Have you played Onimusha: Way of the Sword and Phantom Blade Zero? Which one do you like best? Clash blades in the comments section below.

Robert (or Rob if you're lazy) is an assistant editor of Push Square, and has been a fan of PlayStation since the 90s, when Tekken 2 introduced him to the incredible world of video games. He still takes his fighting games seriously, but RPGs are his true passion. The Witcher, Persona, Dragon Quest, Mass Effect, Final Fantasy, Trails, Tales — he's played 'em all. A little too much, some might say.

New ID. AURA is the mainstream EV VW needs – but its software is the real story

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Volkswagen built its global empire on the promise of affordable, accessible transportation, embodied by the iconic Type 1 Beetle. Now facing an existential crisis after a decades-long push upmarket, the automaker faces a reckoning – and a retreat back into affordable, mass-market electric sedans like the new FAW-VW ID. AURA L5 (and the software that powers it) might be exactly what VW needs to reach a new generation of buyers.

Like the Ford Model T, the original VW Type 1 Beetle was an engineering-led answer to a basic problem: making family transportation affordable and accessible to the masses. And regardless of how you feel about Elon Musk, the Tesla Model 3 has become the spiritual successor to those cars, bringing electric transportation to millions of mainstream drivers. As the Model 3 approaches its 11th year in public view, the recently revealed all-electric ID. AURA L5 and VW JETTA looks like it could be a compelling rival to the mainstream M3.

Co-developed by Volkswagen’s Chinese and German design teams and set to be built by the FAW-VW JV, the ID. AURA L5 is an Accord/Camry-sized electric family car a practical fastback-style rear hatch, frameless doors, and a claimed “84.4% usable floor-area ratio.” The power-operated hatchback opens its maw more than 70 degrees, revealing a 39″ wide opening that can handle groceries, luggage, golf clubs, pets, bodies – whatever you got.

Don’t take my word for it, though. This is the original text from the company’s press release (translated from Chinese by Google):

The ID. AURA L5 is a pure electric family sedan, jointly designed by Chinese and German design teams, and highly replicates the design essence of the ID. AURA concept car shown at the 2025 Shanghai Auto Show. Its short overhang and long wheelbase proportions create a sleek and flowing coupe-like stance, achieving a “usable floor area ratio” of 84.4%. The sides feature true frameless doors, semi-concealed door handles, and an electric hatchback tailgate with an opening angle exceeding 70°, and an opening width exceeding 1000 mm, greatly improving the convenience of loading and unloading large items, deeply integrating fastback aesthetics with practicality. Some models will be equipped with LiDAR and feature the Hyper Sense 8 end-to-end large model, making daily travel easier and more convenient for customers.

The production version of the ID. AURA L5 is expected to pack the same 155 kW (~208 hp) electric motors announced when the concept AURA L5 was first shown last year (battery specs were not released), and was shown as part of a broader product announcement of what VW Group China would be showcasing at this year’s China International Import Expo (CIIE). These include the first two models fully equipped with Volkswagen Group’s locally developed VEGA “integrated technology matrix” (read: software), and the Volkswagen Anhui intelligent pure electric “coupe” crossover.

The announcement coincides with reports from outlets like Autocar and Reuters that Volkswagen is evaluating whether to deploy its China-developed electrical architecture across the majority of its European markets.

“According to internal discussions confirmed by Group board member and Volkswagen brand CEO Thomas Schäfer,” reports Autocar’s Greg Kable, “up to 75% of the firm’s core models in Europe could eventually use the China Electronic Architecture.”

The Volkswagen Group has been struggling to deliver a software experience that resonates with customers. To their credit, they’re looking for outside help, spending billions of dollars on a software collaboration with EV startup Rivian for its upcoming electric truck brand Scout rather than going it alone. With billions of dollars and potential future of the company at stake, then, this seems like the most interesting bit of the announcement:

During the event, the immersive interactive experience exhibit “Digital Magic Cube,” which made its debut at last year’s China International Import Expo, will undergo an iterative upgrade to showcase the core strength of CEA (China Electronic Architecture), the software pillar of Volkswagen Group’s integrated self-developed technology matrix VEGA, to the attendees.

To date, CEA has enabled five new models from three joint ventures within the Volkswagen Group on a large scale, and software iterations have already been completed this year. CEA brings three core advantages to the Group’s new model lineup in China: intelligent accessibility, continuous upgrades, and safety redundancy. Through immersive interactive presentations, it provides consumers with the most intuitive experience. Empowered by the new AI R&D toolchain “C-Brain,” CEA introduces AI agents into software development, design, and engineering verification, enabling more flexible and efficient integration and innovation of vehicle intelligence capabilities, giving vehicles powerful thinking and perception abilities. Frontier Hires A Wizz Air Executive As Two Leaders Walk Out

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Frontier announced Monday that Michael Delehant will join as Executive Vice President and Chief Operating Officer (COO) on November 30, 2026 according to AirlineGeeks. He most recently ran operational and commercial activities as a group Managing Director at Wizz Air, and before that he was chief strategy and network officer at Vueling; he also held several leadership roles at Southwest. He will report to Frontier CEO, Jimmy Dempsey.

The other half of the announcement is the exits. Trevor Stedke, Senior Vice President of Operations, and Alex Clerc, Senior Vice President of Customers, will both depart on November 13. Frontier didn’t provide a reason for either. Not all departures have a “spending more time with my family” boilerplate announcement provided and there’s nothing wrong with that. But  it’s telling that the person running operations and the person in charge of customers are both gone, unironically about two weeks before a new operations boss shows up for work.

James Fenner, who has spent more than a decade at Frontier and was most recently Vice president of Revenue and Loyalty, becomes Senior Vice President and Chief Revenue Officer. His job now covers pricing, revenue management, loyalty and network planning. Most of those make sense to me together with the exception of loyalty. Pricing and revenue management go hand-in-hand as does network planning. But with the Frontier getting a dedicated first class, and the loss of Spirit (so far), Allegiant/Sun Country merging, and the struggles JetBlue is facing and Frontier’s own revenue challenges – it feels like loyalty should be its own gig.

Bobby Schroeter becomes Chief Marketing Officer, overseeing the brand and customer experience. With the customers executive leaving, customer experience moves under marketing. I would have thought loyalty responsibilities would have fallen to CMO but I am apparently mistaken.

Frontier lost $104 million in the Q3 2026, compared with a $77 million loss a year earlier. Removing special items and the airline made $40 million on a record $1.36 billion in revenue. Matthew covered it the day the numbers dropped as a loss despite record revenue, and he found reason for optimism.

Dempsey took over after longtime chief, Barry Biffle, left suddenly in December last year and was named CEO in January. This CEO, on the job about 10 months, is swapping out the operations chief, the customer chief and handing the revenue chief and in essence reinventing the carrier. I want to applaud Dempsey for taking his time to evaluate before making such dramatic changes. Newly installed leaders often want their own people and to break from the past to do things their way and make their own mark. However, as long as it has taken to make these changes, many might have presumed that he felt he was able to make those changes with the current staff in place.

Hiring a Wizz Air executive indicates what Frontier thinks its future playbook is. Delehant comes from two low-cost European carriers, Wizz and Vueling, and those two airlines operate differently from each other and the market is different from Frontier. Vueling is owned by International Airlines Group, the same parent as British Airways, Iberia, LEVEL, and Aer Lingus. Vueling operates outside of the network carrier model with connections and oneworld partner feed but still benefits from the incredible buying power of IAG. Wizz does not.

But for those who have called the death of LCCs and ULCCs in the United States with the shut down of Spirit, the new Frontier could fly in the face of that. Further, as pointed out on this site before, based solely on flying people and things, Ryan Air might be the most profitable true airline (not loyalty program) in the world. It closed last year earning $2.4bn, and every carrier that placed above Ryan had a loyalty program and credit card tie-in.

One thing that has become very clear, is that first class in the United States simply must be offered to be successful. Frontier, Allegiant/Sun Country, and JetBlue have all added a first class product this year (JetBlue had Mint for long haul but its shorter haul domestic first class is genuinely new.) Spirit had just moved into this space too, separating its Big Front Seat product into a First Class cabin, then European-style Business Class (blocked middle seat) though the writing was already on the wall in Fort Lauderdale.

These changes at Frontier could foretell the future of the ultra low cost model in the United States, first class and all.

Whether Delehunt’s changes have the capability of increasing Frontier Airlines stock price will likely determine just how much further he goes. But he’s right to install his own leadership, and given the market segment, quite savvy to find executives with experience in the ULCC space and at least some overlap in the United States in Southwest Airlines. Frontier has a credit card, its loyalty division now shares a boss with revenue management, and a hub model at least in part so it’s not a like-for-like fit with Wizz or other ULCC models. But it’s not a miss either, and I am interested in seeing just how much of the Spirit vacuum Frontier chooses to operate.

AI Agents Still Need Middlemen. But Will They Need Amadeus?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: An Amadeus office in Bad Homburg, Germany. Adobe Stock / Peter Brauers

Amadeus has the scale and airline relationships, but those advantages don't guarantee it a direct role in AI-agent bookings.

Amadeus has spent decades connecting airlines with travel sellers. AI agents may change how customers shop, but somebody still has to find flights, complete bookings, and handle changes. On its Q3 2026 earnings call on Friday, November 6, an analyst asked why Meta's Muse was using Duffel rather than Amadeus to do that work.

CFO Carol Borg said the Muse-Duffel partnership supports Amadeus' belief that AI platforms will use aggregators rather than build booking systems themselves. She doesn't think it displaces the GDS model: "It actually reinforces it."

But Duffel, not Amadeus, has the direct relationship with Muse. Other companies are pursuing similar roles across airline distribution, hotel booking and payments.

As Skift reported in September, Muse uses Duffel to access flight inv

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Amadeus used its Q3 2026 earnings call on November 6 to argue that AI platforms will plug into aggregators rather than build their own booking systems, a position CFO Carol Borg said the Meta Muse–Duffel partnership "reinforces." The detail behind that claim is that Duffel, not Amadeus, holds the direct relationship with Muse, even though Duffel's inventory includes direct airline connections, NDC content, and traditional GDS inventory from Travelport. A growing set of intermediaries is taking similar positions: Selfbook powers hotel booking and payments for Perplexity, Nuitée supplies hotel booking infrastructure to Grab and Revolut and is building APIs for AI applications, Katanox has partnered with Selfbook on distribution and settlement, and AirGateway and Kyte offer API-based airline connectivity. CEO Luis Maroto pointed to roughly 3 billion daily searches and said servicing complex itineraries at that scale is harder than connecting to individual airlines. The financials show pricing holding up as volumes slip: bookings fell 2.8% in the first nine months of 2026, which the company attributed largely to Middle East disruption, while revenue per booking rose 5%. Group revenue rose 5.2% at constant currency to €5.08 billion (about $5.67 billion), with adjusted EBIT up 5.5%, and management expects full-year results near the lower end of guidance while maintaining its 2026–2028 targets.

God of War Spin-Off Game Seemingly Getting Surprise PS4 Port

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

God of War: Sons of Sparta, the retro-style Metroidvania by Mega Cat Studios, is seemingly coming to the PS4.

The game first launched on the PS5 as a State of Play shadow drop earlier this year.

Word comes courtesy of the social media account PlayStation Game Size, which frequently scrapes Sony’s servers for unannounced information. It most recently accurately leaked the file size of GTA 6, for example.

Writing on X (or Twitter), it simply said: “God of War: Sons of Sparta - PS4.”

Asked for clarification about whether the spin-off side-scroller is coming to Sony’s last-gen console, it simply replied, “Yes.”

Given the style of game, I imagine Mega Cat Studios would have absolutely no problems porting this title to the PS4 – it’s just surprising it’s going to the effort to do so.

While the last-gen console remains popular, Sony has mostly moved on from it now. The last first-party game it released on the console was MLB The Show 23 in 2023, so it’s been over three years.

Should this turn out to be true, PS4 owners will be getting access to a likeable if largely unremarkable Metroidvania.

In our 7/10 God of War: Sons of Sparta review, my colleague Stephen Tailby wrote:

“God of War: Sons of Sparta is a fun spin-off that takes a series we all know and love and places it somewhere entirely different. It's a mostly successful experiment; the Metroidvania structure works well with the franchise's mechanics, and it looks and sounds the part. However, it takes too long to really come into its own, leaving the early parts of the game feeling a bit lacking compared to the richer experience found later on. If you have the patience to see it through, and can overlook some rough edges here and there, this is a worthwhile adventure for fans that delves into Kratos' history in a way we haven't seen before.”

Assuming PlayStation Game Size is accurate, I imagine an announcement will be made before the end of the year.

Missing teenager rescued after reaching out to another player in Among Us

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

"Our priority in this case was the safe return of the minor victim."

A teenager who went missing in Utah more than 17 months ago has been rescued from a home in Oregon after she got in touch with another young player in Among Us.

According to a federal criminal complaint reported by Newsweek and KUTV (via IGN), the girl, who was 15 when she disappeared from Garden City, Utah, on 29th May 2025, began chatting with another teenager in Among Us on or around 15th October this year.

The pair later moved their conversation to a different social media app, where she told them who she was and that she was being held against her will in Oregon. She had been given a phone with no cellular data, which meant she could only get online via wi-fi.

The other teen told their parent, who contacted the FBI on 1st November. Agents listened in on a video call between the parent and the girl, and she was recovered from the house later that day. She is now 17.

Adam Hunter Harmel, 39, of Huntington, Oregon, was arrested during a traffic stop.

According to the US Attorney's Office for the District of Utah, he allegedly met the girl online in May 2025, then drove to Utah to pick her up and took her back to his home across state lines. He was charged on 2nd November with transportation with intent to engage in criminal sexual activity, and made his first court appearance in Boise, Idaho, on 4th November.

"Our priority in this case was the safe return of the minor victim," said US Attorney Melissa Holyoak. "Those who allegedly exploit and prey upon the most vulnerable members of our communities will be relentlessly investigated and held accountable."

Utah's US Attorney's Office stresses that a complaint is merely an allegation, and that all defendants are presumed innocent until proven guilty in a court of law.

Following Assassin's Creed Shadows' and Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced's success, Ubisoft is apparently struggling to strike while the iron's hot. Its next mainline game in the series - codenamed Assassin's Creed Hexe - has reportedly been delayed internally, while the publisher works on two other AC games.

AC Hexe was actually announced alongside Shadows more than four years ago, though we haven't had a concrete look at the supposedly witchy, spooky open-world game since. All we knew for sure is that Hexe would launch sometime after Shadows, and here we are now.

Insider Gaming now reports that Assassin's Creed Hexe ran into further development road bumps and has since been internally delayed by another year, citing anonymous sources in the know. According to the report, the likelier release date for Hexe could be sometime in 2028.

AC Hexe hasn't had the smoothest journey to launch so far, with or without another internal delay. The game's original creative director Clint Hocking left the company earlier this year, with Black Flag and Origins' lead developer Jean Guesdon taking over for him. Two months later, game director Benoit Richer also departed.

Still, Ubisoft's purportedly cooking up more than one dish in its historical and historically stealthy-stabby series. Well connected YouTuber j0nathan recently claimed there are two other codenamed AC games in development - Assassin's Creed Mars and Assassin's Creed Pocket.

AC Mars supposedly takes the series to Ancient Rome for the first time, perhaps tying into the upcoming Netflix show in some way. Assassin's Creed Brotherhood did take place in Rome during the Italian Renaissance era, so there's probably fun ways for the lore to connect if Ubisoft did ever parkour down this route. On the other hand, Pocket is apparently set in Jerusalem and is said to be a mobile game, hence the name. As always, take any rumors with a handful of salt until Ubisoft confirms plans for itself.

So, there's lots of Assassin's Creed flying about the place. To keep up with everything going on, check out every upcoming Assassin's Creed game currently in development.

WoW: Forever is the biggest launch in World of Warcraft history, says Blizzard

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever is the biggest launch in the MMO's history, pulling in more concurrent players than any previous WoW launch.

Blizzard shared the news on social media yesterday (7th November), saying the new edition, which launched on 4th November, broke records for both peak concurrent players and returning players.

"Congratulations everyone, you made history!" the official World of Warcraft account posted. "This is all because of you, the players. From all of us, thank you!"

The game hasn't just tempted back lapsed players. In a follow-up post, executive producer and World of Warcraft vice president Holly Longdale said it brought in the most new players for a WoW launch in 15 years.

“He’s Got The Yips” Mads Hermansen’s West Ham Nightmare Goes Beyond Bad Form

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

There is a phenomenon in sport known as the yips. It’s a condition which seems to affect previously capable sportsmen, psychologically blocking them from performing even the simplest of tasks. In darts, it can become difficult to release the dart from the hand, while in golf it can make putting almost impossible.

In essence, you’re frozen, seemingly unable to perform a task which should have become second nature. And it was during Blackburn Rovers’ second goal against West Ham yesterday, when I watched Mads Hermansen fail to dive, that I found myself wondering whether this goalkeeper has developed the footballing equivalent of the yips.

It wasn’t just that goal, of course. The first was equally concerning, when Ezra Mayers was beaten down the flank and the ball was crossed into the penalty area. The situation required some kind of intervention from Hermansen, but there was none. No punch, no parry, no save. Just indecision and a complete lack of intervention.

Unfortunately, this apparent hesitation has permeated Hermansen’s game recently and it’s hard not to conclude that he’s got the yips. There have been occasions when he has looked like little more than a bystander as goals have flown past him, sometimes appearing rooted to the spot or simply falling over rather than making a convincing attempt to save the ball.

The statistics are equally worrying. Based on the match figures we’ve examined, Hermansen has saved just 20% of the shots on target he has faced over West Ham’s last three Championship matches, conceding eight goals from ten attempts on target.

That’s an extraordinary return for a goalkeeper at any level, let alone one playing for a club with West Ham’s promotion ambitions. And while defenders must accept their share of the responsibility, the goalkeeper ultimately has to provide some resistance when opponents get a shot away.

The warning signs were already there before Cardiff, and his latest performance has only increased my concerns. I don’t know whether Hermansen is suffering from anything resembling the yips, but from watching him, I do wonder whether his confidence has deteriorated to the point where instinctive reactions are being replaced by hesitation.

This has become a huge problem, and one Scott Parker needs to address immediately. As things stand, I would have more confidence in either Alphonse Areola or Finlay Herrick than a goalkeeper who currently appears unable to make the saves West Ham desperately need.

Areola is an experienced international goalkeeper whom Parker already knows from their time together at Fulham. I thought he deserved his opportunity against Blackburn, and nothing I witnessed yesterday has changed my mind.

Hermansen’s performance was among the biggest disappointments in our Blackburn player ratings, and continuing to select him simply because he was previously considered first choice doesn’t make much sense when his performances have deteriorated so badly.

Clearly, there is a bigger issue to address in January, when West Ham will have another opportunity to examine their goalkeeping options. But we’ve only just entered November, and there are still two months and a hell of a lot of fixtures to negotiate before the transfer window opens.

That’s an awful lot of potential points to lose while waiting for a goalkeeper to rediscover his confidence. Parker has an international break in which to make some important decisions, and I would suggest the identity of his first-choice goalkeeper should be very near the top of that list.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

Don’t Get Locked Into a Spring or Fall Marathon Cycle. A Run Coach Explains Why You Should Race in Other Seasons.

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Here’s how to adjust your training timeline for better weather, faster performances, and more motivation.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

When choosing a marathon, most runners focus on the details of the race itself: size, location, weather, elevation, and crowd support. Those are crucial considerations, of course, but they only account for one day, compared with the months you spend training for 26.2 miles. So, you also need to factor in what those months of training could look like depending on the race you choose.

Once you do that, you may realize that racing your marathon in the winter or summer actually benefits you more than racing in the spring or fall. In fact, some runners find that the PRs just out of their reach during those more popular racing windows were simply waiting for them in a different season. The missing piece? Moving the timeline of their marathon cycle to maximize factors like consistency.

A run coach who’s competed in every season explains how to pick a race that sets you up to perform at the right time each year and how flipping your training and racing seasons just might lead to more fun, less stress, and even faster times.

Some of the biggest, most exciting 26.2s occur in the fall and spring—take six of the World Majors, for example. But these marathons can also come with less-than-ideal training timelines, Elizabeth Corkum, a.k.a. Coach Corky, an RRCA-, UESCA-, and USATF-certified run coach, tells Runner’s World.

When race day falls in September, October, or April, you’ll likely have your most important workouts and long runs on tap for the least ideal weather of the summer and winter months: brutal heat and humidity or short days with ice and cold temperatures, Corkum explains. Many runners, though, deem these tougher training conditions a fair trade for a shot at a cool, dry day in the low 50s when they toe the starting line.

Some runners would rather flip the script: Do the bulk of their training and peak mileage weeks in nicer weather, instead of hoping for ideal conditions on race day.

If you don’t enjoy running as much when you’re waking up at 4 a.m. in the summer to beat the heat or waiting until the sun goes down to squeeze in miles just before bed, you may benefit from switching your main training season. Some options to consider might be the California International Marathon (CIM) in December or the Houston Marathon in January, Corkum suggests.

Or, maybe you find yourself worn down from trudging through sleet and slush, bundling up for runs in below-freezing temps, logging miles in the dark when the days shorten, or simply confining yourself to the treadmill because it’s the only place that isn’t slick with ice. In that case, you might want to complete your heaviest training in the spring for a summer race. Some great options to consider are Grandma’s Marathon in June, the San Francisco Marathon in July, and the Gold Coast Marathon in July.

For many runners, the greatest benefit of doing the bulk of their training during the fall and spring is that they enjoy the process more. They’re excited to get out the door each day to put in the miles when the weather is cool and crisp from September through November, or they’re finally catching glimpses of green and spring blooms from March through May.

“The mental impact of just grinding day after day in the bitter cold or brutal heat can sometimes make running feel less fun, and I think there is some mental exhaustion that can come from doing that in summer and winter,” Corkum says. “Some people love it and they thrive on it—but many don’t.”

When runners can train in the conditions they feel their best in, they’re usually able to get their miles in more consistently and the entire marathon cycle becomes more enjoyable, she adds.

Once you make the switch, you may not only find that it’s less challenging for you to adhere to your training schedule but that you also have more mental energy and motivation. While these benefits are psychological, they can lead to real physiological training improvements, too.

Besides the mental boost that comes from logging miles in the spring and fall, you might find that your body responds better to training in milder conditions, as well. This may allow you to train harder and recover more quickly.

“October and November are months during which I sweat less and running is not as taxing,” Corkum says. “My heart rate is 5, 10, even 15 beats lower at the same effort because of the weather. And because of that, all my easy runs are even easier on my body.”

Corkum has found that she can usually handle about 10 to 15 more miles each week, and this higher training load, for her, doesn’t require as much recovery as it would during the hotter months of the year. “There’s a huge benefit if we can add more mileage with less stress on the body. If we’re able to run more, that can make us much better runners,” she explains.

A study of more than 119,000 runners published in Sports Medicine in 2024 backs up Corkum’s recommendation: Higher averages of easy-running mileage were associated with faster race performances. So consider which season allows you to safely and comfortably log more miles each week.

Regardless of the season you choose for your marathon, good weather isn’t guaranteed. Corkum says runners should prepare for the possibility that even races like CIM, which has a strong track record for being cool and overcast, could experience an unexpected pop of heat. So, she encourages runners to incorporate some type of heat training—especially if you’re not getting much exposure to hot and humid temps during runs.

“We don’t need to be running in uncomfortably hot and humid conditions every single day to be ready for a warmer race day. Passive heat training—sitting in a hot tub, bathtub, steam room, or sauna—can help us heat adapt, too,” Corkum says.

Another tip Corkum gives her athletes? Do your research. “Go online to check the average temperatures and dew points of a race for the last 10 years or so. Look at the outliers, too,” she says. This information helps you determine if race day and the training season will offer the conditions you want.

And finally, learn to adapt when—despite your best preparations—race day doesn’t go your way. Ask yourself how you’ll adjust your expectations, fueling, and pacing instead of forcing the effort, because that can change your experience of the day and even your finish time, Corkum says.

The season for your best marathon performance will largely depend on your ability to prioritize the aspects of training and racing that feel most important to you, Corkum explains. So when choosing your marathon, consider how much emphasis you’re putting on both race day and the months of the journey.

“Of course, we all want the perfect day for our marathon. But it’s the months of training beforehand that get us there feeling fit and confident,” she says. Keeping both pieces in mind can help ease race-day pressure and elevate your entire marathon cycle regardless of the time of year—or time on the finish clock.

Morgan is a gear editor who has been with Runner’s World since 2017. She started as an intern ghostwriting The Warmup, a bygone version of the daily RW newsletter. Now, she tests and reviews anything you might find on runners’ feet-from crew socks and compression boots to carbon-plated super shoes.

A lifelong runner and shoe geek, Morgan has been chasing the perfect pair of kicks since she joined her grade school cross-country team. Since then, she ran as a Division I walk-on for the cross-country and track & field teams at the University of Delaware, where she studied English and Biology. She has one full marathon under her belt, and has raced more halfs and 5Ks than she can count.

Raising Cane's Is Expanding In 10 States—See If Your City Made The List

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Your next Box Combo might be a whole lot closer to home.

If you're someone who would happily drive out of your way for Raising Cane's chicken fingers and a side of Cane's Sauce, your next order might be a whole lot closer to home.

The popular chicken chain is expanding in a pretty big way this November, with 12 new restaurants planned across 10 states. For some communities, it'll even be their first Raising Cane's location.

Nation's Restaurant News says the new markets include Brick, New Jersey; Fayetteville, North Carolina; and Sioux Falls, South Dakota, with the chain also adding restaurants in Alabama, California, Connecticut, Florida, Illinois, Michigan, and Utah.

According to USA Today, the 12 restaurants are scheduled to open in the following cities throughout November:

For anyone unfamiliar with Raising Cane's, the chain has built its reputation around a fairly straightforward menu featuring chicken fingers, crinkle-cut fries, Texas toast, coleslaw, and its signature Cane's Sauce. Rather than offering dozens of different chicken sandwiches and menu combinations, Cane's keeps its focus on the essentials.

The chain is also picking up the pace with its openings. After adding seven restaurants in October, it's planning nearly twice as many for November, with the overall expansion as part of a much bigger growth push.

According to NRN, Raising Cane's generated $5.48 billion in sales in 2025, up 10.6 percent from the previous year. Founder Todd Graves has said his goal is to make Raising Cane's one of the 10 largest restaurant brands in the United States.

Personally, I'm just happy more people are getting easier access to that Texas toast and Cane's Sauce.

Using miles to fly for Thanksgiving? These are the most expensive and cheapest routes

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Airline ticket prices have surged this year, especially for travel over the holidays. Don't expect many bargains if you're booking flights with miles, either.

Tickets bought with frequent flyer miles for flights between large U.S. airports over the Thanksgiving travel period will cost 32% more than last year's holiday, according to data from Seats.aero, a miles and points redemption search site. While fares have skyrocketed this year, airline executives last month said demand remains strong.

Airlines have largely gotten rid of static mileage redemption charts in favor of dynamic pricing, making budgeting for an award ticket more difficult. Dynamic pricing means redemption costs change depending on demand, routes and other factors.

Many of the most expensive routes this Thanksgiving are understandably longer, including from Hawaii, but the list also includes a nonstop from New York to Houston.

Here's how mileage redemptions for the holiday are stacking up, based on one-way flights on average as of early November and compared with last year:

Jet fuel prices have surged since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran on Feb. 28, which has eaten into airline profits. Carriers have sought to pass costs along to consumers as much as possible.

Even some of the cheapest routes for award tickets are more expensive than last year, including for short routes like Tulsa, Oklahoma, to Dallas Fort Worth International Airport.

Rare fifth-century burials of Germanic chieftains with dazzling weapons and warhorses discovered in France

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Archaeologists found a small cemetery full of high-status burials, two of which may be the graves of Germanic chieftains.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Archaeologists excavating in northeastern France have unearthed a small fifth-century cemetery containing the graves of two Germanic chieftains and two warhorses. The burial ground is providing new information about the transitional period after the fall of the Roman Empire.

The burial complex was discovered near the city of Colmar, just a few miles from the Rhine River, which forms the border between France and Germany. This area had a Gallo-Roman village from the first to the third centuries, and archaeologists found several ancient houses, hearths, wells and craft areas, according to a Nov. 6 translated statement from the French National Institute for Preventive Archaeological Research (Inrap).

Archaeologists also uncovered a rare fifth-century cemetery at the site, which was used from about A.D. 450 to 500, a critical time period in Western Europe because of the collapse of the Roman Empire. While scholars have debated for centuries about why the empire fell, one key issue was the repeated occupation of Rome in the fifth century by Germanic tribes known as the Goths and the Vandals.

Several of the burials included gold-and-garnet accessories on weapons and clothing.

Archaeologists discovered 11 adult graves, three graves of children and two horse burials at the site, according to the statement. In two of the female burials, archaeologists found silver bird-shaped brooches, glass bead necklaces and a bone comb, suggesting that these may have been high-status women.

But two of the male burials, in particular, stood out because of their high-quality grave goods, including a sword with a partially preserved wooden scabbard, a shield, a javelin and a scramasax, a type of Germanic short sword, which was decorated with a gold-and-garnet accessory. The weapons are characteristic of the burials of Germanic chieftains or mercenaries, according to the archaeologists, and two horses were buried nearby with their harnesses.

Two horse burials were discovered in the cemetery.

In addition to the weapons and warhorses, archaeologists noticed evidence on the men's bones that hinted that the individuals were likely warriors. Two people suffered stab wounds that showed no signs of healing, which suggests they died quickly and violently, according to the statement. And another man's right knee bones had completely fused together, which would have prevented him from bending it. This condition, called ankylosis, commonly results from an improperly healed injury.

The archaeologists believe the warrior's grave goods weren't just for show, but further study of the skeletons is planned to confirm whether the injuries seen during the excavation could have been sustained in a conflict.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Few Germanic settlements and cemeteries from the fifth century have been found, so the newly excavated site should provide important information about the upheavals linked to the fall of the Western Roman Empire and how that political collapse affected communities on the edge of the empire.

Can you identify these historical objects of war? Test your smarts with our weapons of the world quiz!

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

The former England international headed stateside in 2021

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After spending more than a decade in the English game, former Arsenal defender Kieran Gibbs decided he was up for a new challenge in 2021.

This saw the 10-time England international head across the Atlantic, where he signed for Inter Miami, in a move that saw him experience and embrace an entirely different footballing culture.

For Gibbs, the opportunity to work with David Beckham and Phil Neville would prove to be a key factor in his decision to up sticks, as was the chance to play a part in a league that was clearly growing.

After spending his whole career in English football, Gibbs was keen to try something new, as he recalls the circumstances that lead to his decision to move across the Atlantic.

“I felt like I wanted to try something new and different,” Gibbs tells FourFourTwo. “Once I spoke to David Beckham and Phil Neville, the game in the US and how much it’s growing appealed to me. There’s a lot of opportunities here. I’d always wanted to play in a different country.”

Gibbs would enjoy two seasons playing in Miami before hanging up his boots in 2022, but remains settled in Florida to this day.

“I’m glad that I picked here because it feels like home now,” he continues. “I go back to the UK when I can, but I’m very settled here. “I’m very grateful that I got to play football in another country and experience a different league.”

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

And while Gibbs clearly has no regrets over this move, he concedes there were initial challenges when he made the move.

“I knew it was going to be a physical game,” he admits. “People mentioned the travel, but I didn’t think that was going to be quite so difficult.

“The heat in Miami is tough as well, when you’re playing through the summer. When I moved here in July, I struggled to adapt to that.”

Gibbs also remains excited about the future of the MLS, believing that last summer’s World Cup will help the sport to continue to grow in the United States.

“It’s a growing league and there’s a lot more to come from MLS over the coming years. The World Cup this year could be a springboard for the future.

“The game has come a long way since the last World Cup here in 1994, when MLS hadn’t even been founded, so it’s still a very young sport. That’s why I’m excited to be here. I want to see how we can keep transforming the game.”

For more than a decade, Joe Mewis has worked in football journalism as a reporter and editor. Mewis has had stints at Mirror Football and LeedsLive among others and worked at FourFourTwo throughout Euro 2024, reporting on the tournament. In addition to his journalist work, Mewis is also the author of four football history books that include times on Leeds United and the England national team. Now working as a digital marketing coordinator at Harrogate Town, too, Mewis counts some of his best career moments as being in the iconic Spygate press conference under Marcelo Bielsa and seeing his beloved Leeds lift the Championship trophy during lockdown.

Who is King Ghidorah? The cosmic connections of Godzilla Minus Zero's foe

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The three-headed Kaiju's origins aren't just space-based; one of the largest structures in the universe shares its name.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Godzilla goes toe-to-toe with long-time foe King Ghidorah in Godzilla Minus Zero, which opened in theaters on Friday (Nov. 6). Though the three-headed golden Kaiju might seem like a natural native of Monster Island, King Ghidorah actually emerged from the depths of space.

However, it isn't just the origins of King Ghidorah — also known as Monster Zero in the Godzilla movie Invasion of Astro-Monster (1965) — that are this beast's only cosmic connection. One of the largest structures in the universe, the King Ghidorah Supercluster, was named in honor of Godzilla's primary antagonist.

King Ghidorah is a three-headed, twin-tailed golden dragon from the stars, first introduced in the fifth Godzilla movie, Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster (1964).

In this first appearance, part of the Shōwa era (1964–1975) of Godzilla history, Ghidorah ravaged Venus, wiping out its population 5,000 years before arriving on Earth encased within a highly magnetic meteorite. Ghidorah was bested this first time by Godzilla, Rodan, and Mothra.

Undeterred, Ghidorah returned for a rematch just a year later in Invasion of Astro Monster (1965), after being discovered supposedly terrorizing the inhabitants of Planet X, a world found beyond Jupiter. In this movie, it is revealed that Ghidorah is less a king and more a pawn, under the control of calculating Xiliens as part of a plan to subjugate Earth. This will become a theme.

Freed from that control, Ghidorah returns to deep space, once again falling under the thrall of an alien race in 1968's Destroy All Monsters.This movie sees Ghidorah hold its own against a who's who of kaiju, eventually succumbing to the combined might of Godzilla, Minilla, Mothra, Rodan, Manda, Gorosaurus, Anguirus, Kumonga, Baragon, and Varan.

Even with three gravity-beam-blasting heads, that is hardly fair!

Ghidorah returned with some backup for a final time in the Shōwa era in 1975's Godzilla vs. Gigan. This time, Ghidorah and his tag-team partner Gigan (who resembled a giant one-eyed space chicken) were under the control of cockroach-like Nebulans. Getting mind-controlled three times is just embarrassing!

Fortunately, the Shōwa era drew to a close in 1975 before Ghidorah was forced into servitude for a fourth time.

Ghidorah returned in the rebooted Godzilla timeline of the Heisei era (1991–1998) in 1991's Godzilla Vs. King Ghidorah, but its cosmic origins are gone. The Heisei Ghidorah begins life as three genetically engineered creatures called Dorats in the future before being morphed into a three-headed form via nuclear exposure.

Ghidorah was given a spiritual origin in the Millennium era's Godzilla, Mothra, and King Ghidorah: Giant Monsters All-Out Attack (2001), actually serving as a protagonist, helping Mothra in halting Godzilla's rampage.

Ghidorah is once again an extraterrestrial in Universal Pictures' MonsterVerse movie Godzilla: King of the Monsters (2019), being described as having "fallen from the stars" in ancient times before rising to challenge Godzilla.

Now King Ghidorah is back once again in Godzilla Minus Zero, maintaining his extraterrestrial origin story and once again challenging Godzilla to an epic kaiju battle. Those are some of the fictional versions of this golden beast, but what does it look like in reality?

Arguably the most impressive feat of King Ghidorah has been to make itself such an important part of pop culture that astronomers took note. The King Ghidorah supercluster is a collection of galaxies located around 6.7 billion light-years from Earth. Covering an area of the sky equivalent to 19 full moons, as seen from our vantage point, this cosmic Kaiju was discovered in late 2022 by Japanese astronomers using data from the Subaru Telescope.

The King Ghidorah supercluster is comprised of at least 19 individual associated galaxy clusters. It stretches out for an incredible 1.3 billion light-years, meaning the entire Milky Way could fit across it around 12,300 times. That makes it the largest supercluster ever discovered.

To put into context just how monstrous the King Ghidorah supercluster is, consider the fact that it has a mass equivalent to 10,000,000,000,000,000 suns, 10 times as massive as the Milky Way's home supercluster, Laniakea. Even Godzilla may think twice before attempting to tussle with that particular King Ghidorah!

See King Ghidorah in action in Godzilla Minus Zero, which is showing in theaters in the US and UK from November 6. You can see the preceding movie, Godzilla Minus One, on Netflix.

Aviation’s Next Disaster Could Be In Your Carry-On

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Battery incidents are rising and airlines are tightening restrictions, with cabin crews the last line of defense. Is the industry doing enough to prevent the next fire, or simply getting better at putting them out?

The culprit was small enough to fit in a pocket, but the consequences were big enough to turn a Boeing 777 around over the Atlantic.

On September 17, a portable charger caught fire aboard KLM flight 735 from Amsterdam to Curaçao. The crew extinguished the flames and the jet returned safely to the Dutch capital. One person was injured and the incident led to knock-on cancellations, overnight accommodation for hundreds of passengers, and the plane being out of service until September 20.

An intriguing twist soon followed when Dutch media reported that the device allegedly belonged to Camiel Eurlings, KLM's former chief executive. The airline has declined to identify the passenger and Eurlings did not respond to Skift’s request for comment. On November 4, the Dutch Safety Board opened an independent investigation into the incident.

Tags: air safety, airlines, jet stream, skift feature

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

A portable charger fire aboard KLM flight 735 from Amsterdam to Curaçao on September 17 forced the Boeing 777 to turn back, injured one person, triggered cancellations, and kept the aircraft out of service until September 20. Dutch media reported the device allegedly belonged to former KLM CEO Camiel Eurlings; the airline declined to identify the passenger, and the Dutch Safety Board opened an investigation on November 4. The incident sits inside a sharply worsening trend. The UK Civil Aviation Authority estimates the average passenger now carries four lithium-powered devices, and its reports of passenger devices overheating rose 98% between 2024 and 2025. The FAA logged 97 lithium-battery incidents involving smoke, fire, or extreme heat in 2025, against 47 in 2017, and deputy director Paul Serafin said the agency has recently seen roughly two incidents a week on passenger aircraft. UL Standards & Engagement estimates an average cost of about $96,000 per event, with about 12% of incidents in its dataset resulting in a diversion or emergency landing. New ICAO provisions that took effect at the end of March cap passengers at two power banks and prohibit recharging them in flight, but implementation varies by jurisdiction and airlines can set stricter limits. Battery safety expert Paul Christensen said the airline industry needs to wake up as a matter of extreme urgency; IATA's Jonathan Jasper cautioned that most reports involve overheating rather than full thermal runaway.

Binance France emails announce closure of its users’ accounts on Dec. 31

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, company says.

Binance France is closing up shop in the country. Customers have been notified by email during early November, according to media reports.

All accounts will be closed on Dec. 31, and any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, Cryptoast, Crypto Briefing and other media reported on Saturday, citing emails sent to users by Binance.

On that date, accounts still maintaining a balance will pay a monthly maintenance fee of 0.025% of the balance, or a minimum of 10 USDC, a stablecoin tracking the US dollar.

To avoid fees, withdrawals must be completed by Nov. 29, the exchange said.

Related: Binance’s EU services face scrutiny over licensing exemption: Report

Cointelegraph reported in late June that Binance had notified European Union users access to key services would be restricted after the exchange failed to secure Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulatory authorization from a member state before a July 1 deadline.

On Sunday, a Binance spokesperson responded to a Cointelegraph query, saying, “Following the end of the MiCA transition period, Binance is progressively restricting certain services for affected users, in line with the schedule previously communicated to them.”

Though asked specifically about reports similar emails had also been sent to users in Spain and Belgium, the spokesperson made no mention of them.

According to media reports, Binance’s global client base counts at least 300 million customers, while the app was downloaded more than 4 million times in the EU last year.

Kate Hudson’s Nostalgic Bow Tree Is Back for 2026! Here’s How to Get the Look – Without It Feeling Dated

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Kate Hudson’s silver, bronze, and gold bow tree is a nostalgic Christmas look. Experts share how to get it right without it feeling dated.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's official; Kate Hudson’s Christmas tree from last year is still inspiring holiday decorations in 2026.

It's hardly a surprise. After all, the actress (best known for her starring turns in Bride Wars and How to Lose a Guy in 10 Days) kept her Christmas tree simple, classy, and very less is more: think silver, bronze and gold bows, a few ornaments and not much else.

Now, a bow is almost as Christmassy as it gets, whether it’s tied on a tree, a wreath or a present. A nostalgic tree does have one risk, though: if you do it without thinking it through, it can go from warm and charming to dated really quickly. Some experts gave me tips on how you can avoid this, and get Kate Hudson’s Christmas tree look.

For those who have yet to see Kate Hudson's Christmas tree in all its glory, flick your eyes downwards...

A post shared by Kate Hudson (@katehudson)

It feels like a rebellion against the minimalist matchy-matchy (and beige) trend that has been going on for so many years, doesn't it? “People are tired of the same old, store-bought, themed trees,” says Irena Cara, interior designer and co-founder of Nova Wrap.

Reilly Renwick, designer and creative lead of State of the Wall, points to Millennial parents whose kids are now the age they were in the 1990s. Many of them, he says, want to revive “the warm, festive and childlike vibe of a 90s Christmas, as opposed to what people are now calling the ‘sad beige’ of contemporary tree styling.”

So, how best to recreate it? Other than buying some of this beautiful metallic gold ribbon from Amazon. We have a few ideas...

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Hey, we might not have Goldie Hawn as a mum, but when it comes to Christmas nostalgia, a lot of millennials remember our parents' houses being packed with all kinds of stuff, and so much color that it felt cluttered. So, the biggest worry with going with a nostalgic look is the tree looking tacky.

For Renwick, “the biggest challenge for anyone trying to create a nostalgic tree is how to evade the ‘tacky’ accusations.” His answer is to look at what Kate Hudson did with hers. “The whole point of this ‘comeback’ thing is to evoke the festive vibe of the 90s, not to recreate it in painstaking detail,” he says.

Shop Nostalgic Christmas Tree Essentials:

Want the Kate Hudson look? “My advice is to use the metallic bows with restraint, in such a way that they stand out but still leave space for the other decorations and the tree itself to still be visible,” says Renwick.

Cara has practical tips for gold, silver, and bronze. “Use brushed or matte ribbon to take the ‘shiny’ edge off, especially if the metals are varying warm tones. Also, make sure the bows are of varying sizes, to keep things interesting.”

For quantity, she says: “On a tree between 6 and 7 feet, use between 20 and 30 bows.” Lighting matters too: “Warm white lights make metallics look even better.”

A Christmas tree runs the risk of feeling tacky, true, but it can also feel too generic, like it should be in a department store in the 90s. Nadi Melam, founder of Florals Memories, has a way to avoid this.

“You can’t purchase nostalgia, but you can tie it!” she says. Her suggestion is to reuse ribbon that already means something. “Use ribbons that have a story, such as paper that a bouquet you received came wrapped in. On the loose end of the ribbon, write down the date you received it, and now your tree is a timeline.”

Melam preserves flowers for a living, so she also suggests pairing a ribbon with a flower from your own garden. She says you can build the look slowly: “You can add one more ribbon to your tree each year.”

If you already have tons of ornaments that have been passed down by family or maybe something your kids have made, bows can tie them into a whole scheme.

Cara says to put the old pieces where people look first: “Place heirloom and vintage pieces at eye level in the center where people look first.” Then, let the ribbon link everything together. “Match the ribbon to details on the older ornaments, such as a gold edge or a red glass.”

A big advantage of a bow tree like Kate Hudson's is that it’s very cheap to update, as ribbon is super easy to swap out for different colors and patterns. “Swap out the ribbon and keep the ornaments to change decorations year to year for a small cost,” says Cara.

Melam says to store ribbon carefully between seasons: “Moisture and light will cause your ribbon to fade, so store it flat in a cool, dark, dry place.”

Kate Hudson’s tree works because it feels warm and traditional without trying to look like a time capsule. Keep your color palette tight and give your ribbon some history to recreate the same nostalgia.

Physicists listened to electrical noise to crack a superconductor mystery

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Tuning in to electronic noise can tell us whether electrons have paired up or not.

Superconductors carry electricity without resistance, but most only work a few degrees above absolute zero. In the late 1980s, copper-based materials called cuprates were found to superconduct at higher temperatures—reachable with affordable liquid nitrogen. Yet nearly 40 years later, physicists still don’t agree on why.

In a recent Science study, a team of physicists led by Milan Allan of Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich and Ingmar Swart of Utrecht University in the Netherlands has found some answers by listening to the faint electrical crackle of current flowing into a cuprate.

Central to the mystery of cuprates’ high-temperature superconductivity is a strange state called the pseudogap. “The pseudogap is a phenomenon of missing electrons,” Allan said. Above the temperature at which cuprates become superconducting, some of their electrons seem to vanish—measurements find far fewer of them at the energies where they should be. “Everybody knows that from different probes, you see that there’s these vanishing electrons, but nobody knows why,” Allan said.

Over the decades, physicists have put forward two main competing explanations. The first hypothesizes that the electrons get stuck in some sort of structure. In this view, electrons freeze into patterns, such as charge density waves, and superconductivity develops separately. The alternative explanation tightly links this phenomenon to superconductivity. “Other people think they’re missing because they form pairs,” Allan said. Pairs of electrons, called Cooper pairs, are what carry current in a superconductor. If, as the material cools, these pairs appear before the material becomes superconducting, they would be a precondition of superconductivity, a waiting room of sorts, where pairs exist but can’t yet move together in lockstep.

Settling this proved extremely hard. “Lots of different experimental signatures are kind of lumped inside this,” Allan said. Different instruments all see something, but their readings, Allan said, are never conclusive. “It’s always something kind of incoherent, hard to grasp,” he said.  What the researchers needed was a way to see pairs directly, and Allan and his colleagues figured it out.

The team decided to listen to shot noise—tiny fluctuations caused by the arrival of individual charges that form the stream of an electric current. Allan says it resembles rain drumming on the roof.  “If you imagine that you have raindrops and then all of a sudden you make the drops twice as heavy, there’ll be more noise,” Allan said. “With the electrons it’s similar. If you have two electrons coming always together, because they’re associated with pairing, then it will be more noise.”

The researchers did this measurement with a scanning tunneling microscope, which passed current between a needle-sharp tip and a cuprate sample, letting them listen to the noise at specific energy levels and different locations on the surface. In conventional superconductors, pairing produces a clean doubling of the noise. In cuprates, the signal is much weaker, because single electrons keep flowing alongside the pairs.

Making the instrument sensitive enough to discern the pairs took years. “What it took is very talented and persistent students who worked for, I don’t know, four years on this to get it working,” Allan said. “There was not a single trick. It was electronics craftsmanship.”

The team examined crystals of a cuprate known as BSCCO, cooled to a few degrees above absolute zero, and compared the noise to the energies at which electrons go missing. By adjusting the voltage between the tip of the needle and the sample, the team could choose the energy of the electrons they were listening to, a bit like turning a radio dial. If the pseudogap came purely from frozen patterns, the team reasoned, the extra noise of pairing should only kick in at energy levels associated with superconductivity. Instead, pairing showed up right at the pseudogap energy.

“The fact that there is more noise indicates that pairing is associated with this large gap,” Allan said. The pseudogap’s energy varies from spot to spot on the surface, but wherever the team listened, the extra noise always started rising at the local pseudogap energy. The same pattern held in samples with different chemical compositions.

Ruling out other sources of noise, such as atoms shifting on the surface, took a lot of effort. “Noise is super hard to interpret, and that was basically the thing that we had the most sleepless nights about,” Allan said. The decisive check involved placing the samples in magnetic fields of up to six tesla, which suppresses pairing while leaving the overall pattern of missing electrons largely intact. As the magnetic field grew, the noise dropped back toward the single-electron level, exactly as expected if pairs were behind it.

The strength of electron pairing can be expressed as the energy required to pull a pair apart. Lowering the temperature helps boost pairing because the warmer a material becomes, the more violently its particles jostle and the more pairs get broken. In conventional superconductors, two ingredients needed for superconductivity—pair formation and phase coherence—occur simultaneously at the critical temperature. (Phase coherence is where the electrons start moving together as a fluid.) In cuprates, Allan and Swart’s study points to a different sequence.

The pairing energy the team measured is well above the temperature at which the material superconducts. “So the picture that emerges is that you first have pre-formed pairs, and then only at lower temperatures, phase coherence sets in,” Swart said. That suggests the superconductivity bottleneck is not getting electrons to pair, but getting the pairs to move together.

“If you have pairing at such high energies, that means that you might be able to push Tc [superconducting temperature] higher,” Allan said. “This is something people hoped for in the very early days of cuprates, and now everybody’s afraid to even mention that. But to me it gives hope.”

An optimistic reading of the results is that cuprate electrons could theoretically pair at room temperature, and we only need to make them coherent to achieve a room-temperature superconductor. The team, though, thinks that’s a bit naive. “I think Ingmar and I both believe that it’s not going to be that simple, because you have all kinds of other effects that are coupled there,” Allan said.

The researchers also stress that their experiment does not fully answer the central question. It rules out the idea that the pseudogap comes from frozen patterns alone, but not that such patterns are intertwined with pairing. “Whatever the detailed explanation of the pseudogap is, pairing should be part of it,” Swart said.

Because the technique used in the study loses sensitivity as temperature rises, the team couldn’t yet listen above the superconducting temperature, where the pseudogap is usually defined. “Hopefully other groups will do that,” Allan said. “What limits Tc, especially if the energy of pairing is high? What happens at Tc? That’s for me the most interesting question.”

Swart wants to make noise maps of the surface with higher resolution and, eventually, an instrument that can detect coherence directly, which he hopes should give us separate markers for the onset of phase coherence and electron pairing. Allan, meanwhile, increasingly thinks the route to better superconductors runs through chemistry, aided by AI, rather than pure physics. “When it comes to trying to make the superconducting material better and increase Tc, I have more hope in direct trial and error,” he said.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Why James Morrison and West Brom fans are tipping 26-year-old star for a Premier League rise as impeccable form continues

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

West Brom sit 5th in the table after a 3-0 win over Millwall on Saturday made it six games without defeat. Isaac Price and Jayson Molumby made the headlines as they both scored one and set up another, but it was a fantastic team performance from WBA who strengthen their promotion credentials with every passing match.

Although not a regular on the scoresheet, one man never far away from praise is Ousmane Diakite. And, once again, the Malian midfielder was excellent on Saturday, playing the full 90 minutes in midfield as the Baggies stormed to victory.

Focusing on the win over Millwall, Diakite was key in his usual role of winning duels, guarding the backline and keeping the ball moving. Primarily operating on the left of the midfield two, the West Brom man completed 51 of his 58 passes, five of which were into the final third while one created a chance.

In duels is where the 26-year-old found most of his success though, as has been the case all season. Diakite won seven duels in total: six on the ground and one in the air. He also completed three tackles, made two interceptions and four recoveries (FotMob).

This latest effort contributed to his eye-catching totals across the Championship campaign to date. No central midfielder has won more than his 92 duels, and he ranks amongst the league’s best in his position for aerial duels won (28 – top 2%), clearances (44 – top 2%) and defensive contributions (98 – top 3%).

On the ball, WBA star Diakite has completed 595 passes (top 9%), made 86 line-breaking passes (top 23%), made 13 successful dribbles (top 8%) and won 23 fouls (top 4%). As far as his stats go, he has been one of the Championship’s most effective midfielders of the season so far in all key areas apart from chance creation and goalscoring with those responsibilities falling to Albion stars Price, Jimmy-Jay Morgan, Aune Heggebo and more.

After a challenging start in a West Brom shirt, Diakite proved his worth in last season’s relegation fight under James Morrison and deservedly earned a new deal. He has got better and better in the 2026/27 campaign, and is a crucial part of an Albion side that looks ready for a promotion challenge.

In the build up to the Millwall game, Baggies boss Morrison was asked whether Diakite was a Premier League player in the making. Speaking to reporter Steve Tudgay, he said: “Yes he is. With us!”

Similarly, former West Brom man Paul Robinson recently told the Express and Star: “He has Premier League attributes, when you look at him”.

Not only do Diakite’s stats back this up, but so do the rave reviews of WBA supporters. Over the past week or so, numerous fans have shared their belief in his potential on X:

Ousmane Diakite WILL be a premier league footballer. Hopefully with us but time will tell #wba

Diakite walks into a bottom half Prem team all day for me #wba

Diakite could get into the Man united midfield all day, his improvement since joining has been unreal to watch #wba

West Brom fans will be hoping Diakite’s form continues. If it does, he may well get his deserved step up to the top flight at The Hawthorns.

Are Tree Saddles Really Better Than Treestands for Hunting Whitetails?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 12:01 PM EST
