Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Early in my bowhunting career, I set up a ladder stand on top of a ridge that had oaks and plenty of deer sign. On opening morning, I watched a deer parade go down a trail that was 20 yards out of range. I sat in that stand several more times and never had a deer come within bow range. If I had a mobile stand, I could have easily adjusted my hunting strategy to move closer to the active trail.

Mobile hunting for whitetails is all about making aggressive moves and constantly hunting fresh spots. Mobile hunters carry everything they need for a hunt so they can set up on the fresh sign and easily adapt to the conditions. That’s why they need lightweight and easy to use hunting platforms.

That old faithful ladder stand just won’t cut it when you need to slide another 100 yards closer to a bedding area. A mobile setup is also great for public land where you can’t or don’t want to leave your stand. It’s also less expensive than buying multiple permanent stands.

There are three main choices for the mobile hunter: a climber, a hang-on stand, and a tree saddle. I’ve hunted from all three of these platforms in my search for the perfect mobile hunting set up. To help with this comparison, I also reached out to Aaron Warbritton, from The Hunting Public YouTube channel. He’s been at the tip of the spear of the mobile hunting trend and offers a wealth of experience. Together we’ll see how each platform stacks up against each other in five categories: weight, setup, huntability, comfort, and price.

A heavy pack can feel fine for a few minutes, but when yards turn to miles, you feel every pound. For hunting deep in public land, a lightweight set up is a must. Weights will differ depending on the model of stand and climbing method. These weights will give you an idea of what you can expect a complete kit to weigh. Here’s how all three systems compared:

Model: Tethrd Mantis Saddle and Predator platform.

Kit Includes: Saddle, tether, platform, lineman’s belt, and three climbing sticks.

Total Weight: 14.1 pounds. *The three climbing sticks separately weigh 7.5 pounds.

Kit Includes: Stand, safety harness, lineman’s belt, and three climbing sticks.

Total Weight: 23.9 pounds. *The three climbing sticks separately weigh 7.5 pounds.

If weight is your primary concern then a saddle is the clear winner. You can even make the saddle lighter by using a lighter climbing system.

Weight isn’t only a factor when carrying a stand, it also matters for setting it up. This is especially true when hanging a saddle platform or hang-on stand. When you climb to the height you want to hunt, you have to hold the stand or platform in one hand while tightening the stand’s straps with the other. A light platform for a saddle, like the Predator platform (3.3 pounds), is much easier to hang then the heavier base of a hang-on stand (12.2 pounds).

“I would say that now 90 percent of my hunts are with a saddle and a platform; just because it’s lighter, the footprint is smaller, and it’s faster for me to set up,” Warbritton says.

With a saddle and a hang-on, you’ll need your choice of hang-on stand and a climbing method. I use the Lone Wolf Alpha stand and climbing sticks. The stand and sticks were designed to pack neatly together making it a very convenient and easy to use system. You can climb the tree and hang your stand in one trip with practice and some paracord. I’m pretty slow and careful when climbing and it usually takes me 15 to 20 minutes to climb and get set. The hang-on is fast, but the saddle is slightly faster and easier for me to set up.

A climber is attached to the tree before you climb and doesn’t require climbing sticks. There’s also no platform to hang once you reach hunting height. You can scale a tree fairly quickly with one, and it’s a less technical climb than a saddle or platform. However, that speed comes at the cost of versatility. Climbers need a straight, limbless tree, which isn’t always available. I much prefer the versatility of other platforms that allow me to climb almost any tree. If you live in an area that has a lot of trees suitable for a climber, then these negatives might not be such a big deal.

The size of a climber is another negative. “I haven’t hunted out of climbers a ton, but I’ve hunted out of them enough to know I don’t want to use them. Their footprint is so big that when you’re packing them in, the stand gets tangled on stuff,” Warbritton says.

Another reason to move away from climbers is the noise they make when climbing. It’s discouraging to put a lot of effort slipping into your spot, but then make a scraping noise as you ascend into position. This is especially true when hunting close to bedded deer.

By comparison saddles and hang-ons are much stealthier to carry and set up. If you are proficient with either of them, you can be up a tree within earshot of a bedded buck without issue.

Proficiency doesn’t come overnight with any of these platforms, but tree saddles especially take time to learn. The saddle has a steeper learning curve because it’s so different than a traditional treestand. The first time you lean back into your saddle it might feel like leaning back on a rocking chair at the edge of a cliff. However, that uneasy feeling quickly fades and I personally feel much safer in my saddle than I ever felt in a hang-on or climbing treestand. That security comes from the fact that if you fall off your platform it is extremely easy to get back on because the tether is always pulled tight—making the drop almost unnoticeable.

Beyond the saddle’s learning curve, the hang-on and saddle are neck and neck when it comes to packing, setting up, and hunting out of them.

“They’re really close. They definitely have their differences. Whichever one that you decide to use, you need to practice with it. Whether you’re using climbing sticks or strap on steps, you need to practice packing, hanging, and then shooting out of them because they’re different,” Warbritton says.

When the rut kicks in and you basically live in a tree all weekend, a comfortable stand is a must. While all three mobile options are pretty comfortable, the standard treestand seat is hard to beat. Warbritton agrees with that preference.

“The only thing I like about a treestand is if I’m going to sit all day, I’d rather have a treestand,” he says.

Tree saddles are comfortable, but a lot of the comfort in a saddle depends on your tether height because it controls the weight distribution. You’ll need to find a sweet spot that works for you and that takes some practice. I haven’t found that sweet spot for comfort just yet, but I do think that I’ll eventually get the saddle to be as comfortable as my treestands.

Read Next: The Best Tree Stands, Tested and Reviewed

Like weight, the price will depend on the manufacturer and model. Just like our weight evaluation, we’ll compare the products I have personally owned and used.

A Tethrd mantis tree saddle, platform, lineman’s belt, tether, and a set of Lone Wolf climbing sticks will run you around $650. Without climbing sticks the system is about $500. If you want to add some accessories like Ropeman ascenders, pouches, and a recliner, you’ll be looking at spending around $900. It’s not a cheap investment, and you’ll have to decide if the above-stated benefits outweigh the cost.

Hang-ons and climbers are less expensive. A premium climber like the Summit Explorer SD and Hunter Safety System safety harness will run you around $450. A Lone Wolf Alpha hang-on, climbing sticks, Hunter Safety System safety harness, and lineman’s belt will set you back around $500. Again, prices will vary depending on what models, climbing methods, and add on accessories you choose.

Read Next: The Best Tree Saddles of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

Warbritton and I both prefer tree saddles because they are lightweight, easy to pack, fast to set up, and make mobile hunting easier. Is it the best system for you? You’ll have to evaluate the features against your hunting needs to make that decision. The good thing is, all three systems will give you the ability to be a mobile hunter.

“I just think that either way you’re going to be hunting mobile and you’re going to have a lightweight setup that’s going to allow you to see more country, learn more stuff, and potentially kill more deer,” Warbritton says.

Scott Einsmann is Outdoor Life’s gear editor. He oversees the gear team’s editors and writers who are subject matter experts in bows, knives, hunting, fishing, backpacking, and more. He lives in Richmond, Virginia with his wife and two bird dogs.

This is how Enzo Maresca's tactics have left Elliot Anderson exposed with too much to do, writes JACK GAUGHAN... and why £125m Enzo Fernandez must step up and offer much more to solve midfield riddle

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published: 07:00 EDT, 8 November 2026 | Updated: 13:08 EDT, 8 November 2026

Manchester City spent £320million on overhauling their midfield over the summer. In doing so, they twice broke their own transfer record. This marked the most aggressive focus on a specific need since the lavish outlay on full backs nine years ago.

All signed weeks before the independent commission’s guilty verdicts were announced, Elliot Anderson, Enzo Fernandez and Ayyoub Bouaddi heralded a new era in the City engine room. More energy, vibrancy, dynamism.

Two of them were ready-made replacements for Rodri and Bernardo Silva, with Bouaddi only 18 at the time and purchased as a potential superstar after shooting to global prominence at the World Cup.

But as Enzo Maresca heads into the second international break of the season on the back of three consecutive Premier League defeats, his midfield is the primary area of concern. And has been for a while.

A big fortnight lies in store for City’s manager, whose midfield has been wide open all year. Fine when outscoring opponents – see the 5-3 victory over Sunderland – and it makes for great entertainment yet an obvious gaping chasm when the goals dry up slightly.

The balance has not felt right and Maresca, who has gently batted away persistent questions about the midfield for weeks, must know this.

Elliot Anderson is being asked to do too much and it cost City in the 3-1 defeat at Forest

While studying tapes of all three defeats over the coming days, before City come back after the internationals to host Fulham and then go to Arsenal, he needs to determine a plan moving forward and that will include some tough decisions on individuals and shapes.

Nottingham Forest could have driven a bus through the middle of the park on Saturday night, two goals from counter-attacking situations engineered between the width of the centre circle. Twice the ostensible holding midfielder Anderson tried to nick possession, beaten to it, and City were turned around.

Noises coming from the dressing room suggest that Anderson is effectively being asked to operate as both a No6 and No8 when picked as the deepest of the three, to go and join play high up the pitch. That is Maresca leaning into the England international’s Action Man, everything to everyone style. That also relies on somebody covering for him.

That somebody does not exist in this current setup, Fernandez always too high and wide elsewhere.

So when they lose duels as happened at the City Ground, something the manager openly bemoaned, City are not stable enough to recover.

Liam Delap's fierce finish put Forest 2-0 ahead - and City were left playing catch-up

Club analysts have sat down with Anderson to go over video clips on how best to follow these instructions to the benefit of the team. Watching him, it’s hard not to conclude that this is a quality midfielder being asked to do too much as a single deep midfielder.

England are sweating on the fitness of Marc Guehi as the Manchester City defender battles a hip injury.

Guehi came off at half time of Saturday's 3-1 defeat by Nottingham Forest, with Thomas Tuchel's squad set to link up in London on Monday morning.

With John Stones already out of Nations League ties with Croatia and Spain, Tuchel would be severely light on experience at centre half should Guehi not prove his fitness.

It will produce moments of sheer class – that lung-busting run up the right in Leipzig on Wednesday that bore hallmarks of Kevin De Bruyne, a perfect cross for Erling Haaland’s winner – while also leaving the defence exposed.

An exposed defence lends itself to a heightened chance of individual errors, plenty of those around at the moment, and it was noticeable at Forest that the opening goal came from uncertainty in possession. Josko Gvardiol made the error, eased off the ball by eventual goalscorer Jair Cunha, but the plan cannot be to have the left back with his back to goal and no viable passing options.

When Marc Guehi’s ball went into Gvardiol, Anderson was higher and wider on the right – deep in conversation with Abdukodir Khusanov about how they’d failed to move possession well enough earlier in the sequence – and Rayan Cherki was belatedly trotting back to offer himself.

Fernandez never once enters the frame, even when Cunha is in the box and subsequently wheeling away in celebration. Maresca rejected the suggestion that there is an issue with the team’s build-up on Saturday night.

As Man City look to build from the back in the third minute, Anderson (No 5) is busy delivering instructions to Khusanov (out of shot)...

... it means that when Guehi plays the ball to Gvardiol, he has his back to goal and nobody in midfield to pass to - and is already under pressure

... and with no support, three Forest players swarm and Jair Cunha dispossesses him, leading to him scoring the opening goal

What is clear though is that there is not yet that balance, that this is not yet a unit. Maresca moved to include Bouaddi with Anderson and Fernandez against Brighton – a one-goal defeat – but that acted as detrimental to creativity. If nothing else, Brighton proved that City require either Cherki or Phil Foden on the pitch at all times.

Over the past two games, Fernandez has lurked more menacingly at the back post, offering an additional goal threat rather than just relying on Haaland. That ghosting late run saw him score in Leipzig but for a joint-British record £125million needs to offer so much more. There will be growing calls for Bouaddi’s inclusion as a genuine deep-lying midfielder but it’s unlikely that the teenager can manage the league’s physicality as a single pivot.

The Moroccan has more chance of stamping his authority when alongside somebody else, dovetailing with an Anderson as a two. That leaves Fernandez, whose barnstorming league debut at Old Trafford feels like a long time ago now.

To persist with him, he either dislodges Cherki as the No10 on the promise of far more goals or Maresca drops him back into a more conventional role that allows Anderson to flourish properly.

Something has to give because the unevenness of City’s three is giving the rest of the team uncomfortable jitters.

How African philosophies inspired a new approach to staging film festivals in Manchester

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

See My World, an event highlighting the pan-African thought via film, poetry and dance, builds on momentum sparked by local activists and creatives

African philosophies including exploring how lessons from the past can shape the present and near future have inspired a film festival coming to Manchester.

See My World, a day dedicated to independent creators from across the African diaspora in the north of England, will take place at the Contact theatre on 21 November.

The festival is part of See My World’s year-round programme showcasing pan-African arts, culture and events.

It reflects the determination among creatives and activists in Manchester to build on the legacy of the 1945 Pan-African Congress in Manchester – which connected luminaries of postwar independence movements with a shared vision of liberation – after last year’s 80th anniversary commemorations.

See My World’s CEO and artistic director, Tunde Adekoya, said this month’s film festival was taking inspiration from across the spectrum of pan-African thought, including the works of Kenyan writers John Mbiti, an Anglican theologian who died in 2019, and Ngũgĩ wa Thiong’o, the author of the groundbreaking 1986 book Decolonising the Mind who died last year.

Both writers rejected the colonial conceit that African thought was inferior, stressed the importance of community and saw reclaiming African ways of knowing as essential to a people’s self-understanding.

While Mbiti sought common ground between African religions and Christianity, Ngũgĩ was a Marxist. He argued colonialism was motivated by cultural and economic conquest, and that European powers used language as their sharpest instrument. Ngũgĩ famously abandoned English to write in Gikuyu, a Bantu language mainly spoken in Kenya and criticised the role missionary Christianity played in colonial rule.

Adekoya said: “It’s a day dedicated to pan-African and global majority films from the north of England with exclusive premieres of documentaries we’ve been working on, which weave together expressions of liberation through art forms like poetry and dance.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

“The whole idea is that it’s like a playground in search of strategies of liberation, using the festival as a way to explore different philosophies.

“John Mbiti explores the philosophy and spiritual understandings of time through the concept of Sasa na Zamani – in which the past informs everything that happens moving forward. That reflects how we constantly go back to pan-African histories to inform how we progress.”

Film-makers have been invited to submit films lasting up to 30 minutes from across genres, with no screening fees charged.

As well as submissions, the festival will feature documentaries produced by See My World. They include Homage, a “cinematic love letter to dance” that traces a culture through present-day portraits illustrating dance artists and archival material from Manchester, telling a story of joy, loss, migration, rhythm and resistance. Meanwhile, Poetic Justice explores how pan-African thought and Manchester’s poetry scene speak across decades.

Adekoya added: “The idea is that we would go against the norm of how film festivals are, because when we think about decolonisation, we think about how structures have been created to alienate and exclude communities.

“So we thought our film festival would look more at how we make film accessible and how stories that deserve to be seen get seen. Throughout the day we will have different activities, from exhibitions to explorations of our performances, discussions, Q&As, insights into directors’ processes, and workshops.

“We’ve been looking for literature and concepts that inform us and give us an understanding of who we truly are. And as works from thinkers like John Mbiti have come into our sphere, they have really emboldened us to think differently about our place in the world and what we do.”

See My World film festival runs from 2pm to 11pm on Saturday 21 November at Contact theatre, Oxford Road, Manchester.

$200 billion lost, but here’s why Solana’s tokenized RWA market could be silver lining

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Altcoins have been bleeding more than Bitcoin [BTC], even though the latter is the main driver of the crypto market’s sentiment. In total, altcoins have lost more than $200 billion in capitalization, implying that the euphoria around them was short-lived.

Deducing Binance’s market, over 70% of the altcoins broke below the 200-day moving average (MA). Here, it’s worth noting that this share is quickly approaching the 80% mark. For instance, at press time, Solana [SOL] was trading at $89 with the MA positioned at $97.76.

The broader altcoin market’s weakness has been accelerated by macroeconomic uncertainty. Especially after President Trump’s Republicans lost control of the two chambers of the U.S Congress.

Additionally, the Federal Reserve, European Central Bank, and Bank of England raised interest rates this week. This move discourages borrowing capital, which would be used to invest in risk-on assets like alts.

Both Nasdaq and the S&P 500 fell from their latest peaks too, reinforcing a broader weakness, even in the stock market.

The discrepancy between the current altcoin performance and that of mid-August is evidence of shifting market bias. Between August and September, the cap of altcoins rose by $320 billion. However, since then, it has shed 62.5% of what it gained.

Despite the sharp drop in altcoin market caps, adoption on Solana has been thriving. In fact, the market cap of tokenized real-world assets (RWAs) on the chain hit a new peak level of $4.80 billion.

At the time of writing, tokenized funds led with $3.70 billion, followed by tokenized stocks and commodities at $1.10 billion and $31.90 million, respectively.

These figures are expected to grow even more in the long term. For instance, Backpack [BP] CEO Armani Ferrante, one of the tokenizing platforms built on Solana, stressed opening up the entire global stock market on the Solana mainnet.

However, regulations remain a major hurdle. Especially in the United States after Republicans lost control of both the House and the Senate.

Solana slid after the midterm elections’ outcome, exerting pressure on the altcoin. At press time, SOL was 9.75% away from its 200-day MA. This reading indicated that bears were in control of the market.

Even so, traders are betting on the growing RWA market to help mitigate the downside risk. As such, SOL could be an outlier when the general altcoin market is weak. Bitcoin would also need to be bullish for more confluence though.

Muriuki Lazaro is a on-chain data analyst with a B.Sc. in Data Science. Muriuki specializes in dissecting complex on-chain data into clear and accurate insights for readers in the crypto ecosystem, with a particular focus on Bitcoin.

How I Shot and Edited a Documentary About Bicycle Jousting In Under a Day

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

How I Shot and Edited a Documentary About Bicycle Jousting In Under a Day

Lime Bike Jousting was conceived by Cameron Carnegie-Brown (AKA Camtholomew the First) and Jack Carden, two London creatives who turned a joke about jousting on rental e-bikes into a viral performance art event.

The first joust, held earlier in October, drew hundreds to London Fields and racked up over four million Instagram views. Lime’s response was measured but clear: “While we appreciate the medieval spirit, safety must always come first. Lime bikes are designed for getting around the city, not for jousting. We discourage anyone from attempting similar events to help keep riders and those around them safe.”

The second event went ahead anyway, with thousands RSVPing online, the location released to the public only two hours beforehand to those registered, and initiatives taken to raise money for charity.

At that second Jousting event, on October 31st, 2026, I produced a short film, a mini-doc, coming in at 10 minutes, 33 seconds. It was a light-hearted event, taking place on the afternoon before Halloween, so plenty in the crowd were dressed up in various costumes, some themed, others a little less on-topic, and once the Joust concluded, they headed straight onward into their Halloween celebrations, to their house parties and trick-or-treating nights.

This was a fast turnaround production. I shot with a Nikon ZR and Tamron 28-75 (an ideal photojournalism-adjacent lens, although at one point I remember wishing I had my 15mm for some extreme wide when I was packed into the crowd). At the same time, Amanda Dombroski used a Panasonic camcorder for B-roll and alternate angles.

The ZR is ideal for run-and-gun cinema: incredibly compact, with phenomenal image quality and stabilization that doesn’t feel unnatural. For interviews at a moment's notice, the 28-75 is ideal, and the reach was basically fine for the action, although I could have used a little more at times.

Combined with the camcorder's extreme reach and versatility, I had plenty of material to choose from in the edit, and I think the mini-doc covers the community aspects of the event and context especially well. Amanda’s footage gave me great cut-ins, alternate perspectives of the joust, and overall flavor, all of which were really valuable to the finished short film.

Photographer Elliott Gunn (who previously featured in my first-ever feature-length film "Everything Happens All the Time") stepped in as a sort of presenter, offering the microphone to various "Squires," "Knights," and other attendees. We even briefly interviewed one of the Lime Bikes, which was clad in makeshift armor.

The event was scheduled to begin at 3:30 PM, although the location wasn't announced until a couple of hours before. I arrived at 2 PM, having walked from Colombia Road to London Fields, where an annual Day of the Dead celebration was taking place. This meant I could catch some quieter moments and interviews before crowds arrived, and warm up visually as different elements fell into place.

I left after the event had mostly wrapped up (although some spontaneous reveling unfolded after the main jousting concluded), around 4:45 PM, so Amanda and I had a relatively short window to record a total of 80 minutes of footage between us. I was working in N-RAW, and the camcorder doesn't support it, so the first hurdle in the edit was grading the footage consistently.

The wired microphone was tricky to work with in such fast conditions, and I got tangled in it a few times while draping it over my shoulders when not in use. While it's a non-brand, cheap thing, the ZR's built-in 32-bit float capabilities left plenty of room to refine the audio, and it sounds really quite good, in my opinion.

It was Elliott's first time as a "presenter," and I think he did well, although I do have some notes for him. One moment saw one armor-clad Knight chugging a can of the soft fruit drink he was there promoting, and Elliott held the mic so close it was almost ASMR; I think he handled it really well, and it was a great moment in the film. The Knight then slammed the empty can against his helmet until it crumpled flat.

The edit worked out at 10 minutes and 30 seconds, not too bad, all considered. It covers the event fairly well, although the jousting itself was the hardest part. As soon as the lances struck, other photographers, videographers, and influencers swarmed the downed Knight, and even as they cycled, some camera operators ran past, obscuring the action. At some points I gave up trying to push through other photographers and spent time on the edges shooting the crowd. If I were tackling this as a longer-term body of work, I would have had the energy to work harder on those aspects and outmaneuver the “competition.”

I think my take is less on the action itself and more on the community. I spoke with the lady who built the lances out of foam and soft wood; Elliott and I spoke with some of the Knights and passers-by; and we got a more in-depth look at the event overall, in a more traditional documentary approach.

It's a pure document of the event as best as I could manage, with depth beneath the surface from the diverse interviews and moments. It doesn't end as strongly, but it isn't a narrative arc; there wasn't much pacing or natural rhythm to edit to, so it was makeshift as best as possible. I didn’t want a highlight reel or a collection of disjointed moments, which is mostly what’s out on social media and mainstream platforms right now.

There’s been such a huge response to the jousting phenomenon as a whole, so I think the audience for this mini-doc is anyone who likes these fringe, seemingly spontaneous community events, and who wants to see a little more than what's been shown on the news or in shorter social media snippets. It's currently out on YouTube and Vimeo.

Unfortunately, I couldn't make it to the first Lime Bike Joust earlier in the month because of a prior commitment. Still, I think if I'd been able to make any recordings, I'd have kept that footage in reserve, kept shooting as events unfolded, and then put together something more substantial. Since it's “just” the second one, I think it made sense to treat it as standalone, because while the event itself is unique, it risks becoming “content saturated.”

I noticed some serious camera rigs in the crowd, and I wouldn't be surprised if someone came out with a more complete narrative film in a few months (or longer, depending on how many jousts are yet to take place, I’m keeping an eye on “TalesByTyler”). Without that level of commitment, it’s just a matter of showing up with a camera and a vague outline of an idea, and doing your best to apply your skill to the scenario, the same as I am used to with my documentary photography.

I used various presets and plugins to give the footage a sort of filmic glow and grain, which meant the ZR footage worked well alongside the camcorder. I didn't have a specific look in mind, but I like Lance Oppenheim's "Ren Faire," and I think there's a little taste of that in this. I used proxies in Premiere Pro to speed up render times and exported in 4K, which took quite a while with all those plugins.

Editing was from when I got home at around 6:30 PM, and then it was finished in the early hours of the following day, so taking into account dinner and distractions, I'd say it was a five-hour editing process. Luckily, I've been in a good creative flow, and it was fun to shoot, edit, and publish something like this in such a short amount of time. The whole thing reminded me that you don't need a perfect setup or a grand plan. Most of the time you just need to show up, pay attention, and let the story tell you what the production needs to be.

How to make a detailed 3D landscape using Photoshop, Cinema 4D and ZBrush

Sci-fi and fantasy artist Calder Moore breaks down his process for creating a dark fantasy landscape into clear manageable tasks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In this tutorial, I’ll be doing an overview of the basic steps I take to create a compelling and highly detailed scene, from the basic sketch to rendering and adding post effects. I’ll also go over using basic geometry with displacement to really push the scene’s details and interest efficiently and quickly. I'll be using Cinema 4D, ZBrush, Octane Render and Photoshop (see our guide to the best 3D modelling software, and you might want one of the best laptops for 3D modelling).

Before diving into complex scenes like this, it’s a good idea to grab some inspirational images to build your visual palette; things like the architecture, environment, lighting, composition and mood. For these types of scenes, I like to pre-prep in 3D a bit, building up a library of foliage scatter systems and materials.

This allows me to create these complex scenes much faster and focus on the art, rather than get sidetracked with technical stuff. A fun fact for this scene: it wasn’t originally going to be greyscale. I turned down the saturation during the lighting phase to check values, and immediately got inspired to keep it that way, as it helped push that video was looking for!

Lastly, these scenes can seem extremely daunting to make. I find that the best approach is to write down all your tasks that you need to do to make it: make tower, break tower, make ground, detail ground, etc, so instead of seeing a big task, you see 20 very manageable tasks.

I’ll almost always start with a small sketch, as this allows me to quickly iterate on a few different compositions to see if any work for the kind of vibe I want to have, and also figure out the flow of the scene in general. In this case, I wanted to have an epic tower with that feeling of lonely, forgotten ancient power.

Once I have a sketch figured out for the direction, I go into Cinema 4D to establish the scene. Using basic geometry and sculpting tools, I build out the basic forms for the landscape and the silhouette for the tower.

During this process, I will also quickly establish the camera position. Locking it in early is key, and lets you refine the scene to that shot.

Now that the scene is blocked in, I can refine elements of it. For the tower, I’ll use displacement textures so I can stick to basic geometry. Using symmetry for archways and radial cloners for pillars, I build up the details for the tower elements, then use radial cloning to create the tower.

Now that we’ve built the tower, we can destroy it! Using various tools like the knife tool and voronoi fracture, I add cuts into the geometry. Loosely following the sketch, I then lasso-select portions of it and delete to remove the unwanted portions, making it look like it collapsed.

It’s good to spend a little bit of time on this step to make sure the damage feels natural.

Next, I’ll dive into the terrain element. Exporting that basic geo and bringing it into ZBrush, I’ll then sculpt more of the basic forms, refining the big shapes more. Using displacement texture on this terrain piece, I’ll keep the details light, as that will add a ton later.

I then create some quick UVs for it so the materials’ displacement doesn’t cause too much artifacting, then export back to C4D.

Now that we have our sculpt back in C4D, I’ll start building up the shader for it. Using a tileable photoscanned rock material, from RD Textures (now in Megascans), I build up a basic shader.

Cranking up the displacement scale for it, I’m able to add a ton of complexity and depth to that basic sculpt. Playing with the scale and UV offset settings, I can then find an interesting silhouette.

We have a basic terrain now, but the ground doesn’t quite feel natural, and this is where the power of displacement textures shine. I’ll duplicate the terrain geo and bring it up in Y a bit and sculpt some areas back down, then assign it more of a rocky ground/ photoscanned material instead of a cliff material.

In the render, you can see how we start to get a nice blend between the materials!

For the tower, I’ll start by creating a basic stone wall material – also with displacement – which helps a ton to add the details that the basic geometry lacked. To help break up that tiled stone material, I’ll then use a noise material that darkens areas to create some grime, and blend it into the material using the gradient marble texture so there’s more grime near the bottom of the tower.

This step is actually done in tandem with steps 7 and 8. For the lighting, I brought in a few HDRs to find a lighting scheme that I felt fit the vibe, and pushed the composition to be stronger, playing with light and shadow in the scene to create a stronger contrast, readability and visual interest.

Lastly, I added a cloud plane with intent to help the scene’s compositional flow.

Making many of these scenes I have a bunch of Octane Scatter systems set up with different trees, shrubs, grasses and plants. With these, I’ll take the terrain, and paint a vertex map to tell the scatter systems where to generate the foliage. In this case, I used my pine trees, shrubs and grass.

The last step in C4D is rendering out the final scene. With these heavy nature scenes, I usually work in portions so that I can see the results faster. During this step, I focus on adjusting the render settings and scene optimisation so I can get a big render fast.

Along with some last-minute scene tweaks, I’ll also render out some passes. These include a Z Depth pass, a Bloom pass and an object ID pass.

With everything rendered, I bring it all into Photoshop. First, I’ll go through the scene, and using basic brushes, paint over the artifacts from displacement and weird areas that don’t read nicely.

For this scene, I wanted to make that ‘old photo’ look, so I played with the levels and exposure to bring up the blacks, adjust the BG values and add that bloom pass.

Here’s a close-up of how I took the base rendered geometry and painted over it, removing those geometry artifacts, smoothing out some visual noise and adding brick details in the silhouette. You can also notice that I added a bit of blur to the BG parts of the tower. This helps the FG ones read a bit better and feel less flat.

Lastly, I added a couple old photo and paper textures on vivid light and multiply (though I played with the layer styles to find a good look), and on top of everything, did some last-minute levels adjustments to balance the scene with those textures layered on.

I also like to duplicate and merge everything down, then add a grain effect. This help get rid of that 3D look.

For more inspiration, see our Blender tutorials.

The Wonder—and Weirdness—of Exploring a National Park in the Dark

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

I’ve spent the majority of my life doing outdoor activities: camping, hiking, biking, kayaking, you name it. It’s intrinsically part of who I am, something that defines my siblings and I so deeply it feels like it’s braided into our DNA.

Perhaps that’s because, when I was just two-and-a-half years old, my parents took my siblings and me on a two-month-long road trip from New York to Arizona, hitting dozens of state and national parks on the way. We still talk about that adventure, lovingly referred to as the Out West Trip. It was the first time I visited Utah’s national parks, known as the Mighty 5: Zion, Arches, Canyonlands, Capitol Reef, and Bryce Canyon.

Once night falls in Utah's national parks, most travelers head back to their campfires or motor lodges—except for those willing to brave the dark and explore their nocturnal side.

A lot has changed since I first visited. Now, millions of visitors head to Southern Utah every year, flocking to these five well-known national parks in addition to the whopping 46 state parks across the whole of Utah. Even in the staggeringly hot and dry summer season, these protected areas teem with tourists from all over the world—Zion alone reported over 1.4 million visitors in June, July, and August of 2026, while Arches reported over 445,000 in the same season.

Once night falls in the Utah desert, though, the temperatures cool off, most tourists head back to their campsites and motor lodges, and the stars shine through that clear desert sky. All of the Mighty 5 are certified dark sky parks—Utah, in fact, boasts the highest concentration of certified International Dark Sky Places in the world. So maybe it's no surprise that, in recent years, these destinations have embraced night-centric recreation, showcasing a lesser-known version of themselves for those willing to brave the dark.

In Capitol Reef National Park, the The Temple of the Sun summit stands against a sky full of stars.

It's why, nearly 30 years after my first Utah park trip, I decided to return—this time, experiencing Southern Utah’s state and national parks once the sun set. I’m a night owl, but I also wanted to revive that sense of wonder I felt so long ago when I first visited. I knew the parks by day, but by night? They were brand new to me. And so, in late September, my friend Sarra and I plotted a week-long road trip linking four national parks and three state parks in Utah. Our goal was to take it easy during the daylight hours with a gentle hike or two, and store up our energy for a nocturnal expedition come sundown. We wanted to get to know Utah’s parks in a way that too few do.

It’s 9 p.m. when we walk into Capitol Reef National Park with hiking guides Samuel and Suzanne Francis, the married owners of Western Whiptails Adventures. The sun has set by a couple hours; my friend and I rely on flashlights, while our guides trust themselves in the dark. As we begin on the trail, I’m struck by the silence surrounding us, serene in a way I’ve experienced only in the redwoods, where the massive trees cradle you against human noise; by day, the stone of Utah’s parks almost reverberates with the many overlapping voices of the crowd. Now, there is no sound left to bounce.

The view of the Hickman Bridge in Capitol Reef National Park at midnight.

Our goal is to hike to Hickman Bridge, a natural sandstone arch—completely in the dark. It’s a 1.8-mile round-trip that begins with an initial climb on switchbacks, flattens out in the middle, and then resumes a narrower uphill path with a bit of a scramble required to reach the view. It’s a hike that should take about 1.5 hours in daylight. Sam says he and Suz have led hundreds of guided tours throughout the park and surrounding public lands, but nothing quite like this. “Capitol Reef’s landscape takes on an entirely different character at night,” he says.

I feel the ground under my sneakers shift from hard-packed dirt to softer sand as we set out, and the herbal scent of pinon and Utah juniper trees tickles my nose. We’re surrounded by dust and desert, but the area feels alive with the chirping of bugs and rustling of leaves.

Suz leads us to little-known petroglyphs, then points out a site where the Fremont people once stored corn. Sarra and I shine our flashlights and look on in admiration. It’s a respite in the adrenaline-pumping stretch of the hike—we have gained a couple hundred feet of elevation, and I am imagining the drop-offs that I’m quite sure lie beyond the tiny circle of my flashlight. My grip gets tighter and I begin to sweat through my layers, realizing that the dark can't quiet my fear of heights. Suz is up ahead, climbing like it’s second nature: “If you turn your flashlights off, your eyes will adjust,” she says, turning back. I laugh, knowing there's no way I can get myself to follow her advice.

The nearly full moon directly over the sandstone summit in Capitol Reef National Park

A hard-to-find petroglyph site in Capitol Reef National Park

“Many animals in Capitol Reef are nocturnal or crepuscular, especially during warmer months,” Sam says, listing off the species we share the night with as we plod on. “Bats, kangaroo rats, desert cottontails, foxes, desert bighorn sheep, mule deer, or even the rare ringtail.”

Once we reach flatter ground, I concede to turn off my light, blinking to let my eyes adjust. I realize the nearly full moon above is blindingly bright—and yet we can still make out hundreds of glittering stars sharing the sky. “The darkness really offers a unique connection to the landscape,” says Sam. I can't help but agree.

And then we finally catch sight of the stone bridge, bathed in moonlight and backlit by stars, appearing especially mammoth in the low light. We sit beneath it, the silence wrapped around us, the space above looking unimaginably infinite. “I’ve seen this bridge plenty of times during the day,” says Suz, agreeing with me, “but at night, it's even better.”

The starry sky shines over Dead Horse Point State Park, where after-dark activities include scorpion spotting and stargazing.

While Capitol Reef's night hike was nothing short of thrilling, another type of excitement awaits us at Dead Horse State Park. The park is known for its dramatic red-rock canyon vistas and hiking trails that cross them—and yet the evening programming is just as robust, with scorpion searching, stargazing, sunset walks, and astronomy talks all included in the $20 entrance fee. Unlike Capitol Reef, where we were possibly the only visitors on the grounds that night (despite it being open 24 hours), Dead Horse is known for its nocturnal programming (much of which begins around 8 p.m., and runs until the park closes at 10). According to Ally O'Rullian, public information officer at Utah State Parks, Dead Horse Point naturalist programs have “likely included dark sky viewings” since as early as the mid 1970s, and this new slate of activities was added to the regular calendar in 2015 as part of its application for International Dark Sky status. We are far from the only ones out tonight—it feels as though this is how the Dead Horse is meant to be appreciated.

A group of travelers gather around a state ranger as they prepare for a scorpion search

We've opted to go scorpion searching, which is exactly as wacky as it sounds. Scorpions are biofluorescent, meaning they emit a visible bluish glow under UV lights. And so we gather at the Visitor Center, where ranger Jimmy Burch is handing out special flashlights. This is one of the most popular activities at the park, Burch says, and perhaps unsurprisingly, the one with the greatest youth turnout. We do feel like kids, as our group of a dozen or so of all ages start down a gentle, meandering path, shining our torches on brush and rocks to see if we can find anything hiding among them. Shine a UV light directly on a bush with a scorpion in it, Burch says, and it would be nearly impossible to overlook the glowing body within. “If you hear hissing, step away,” he cautions, as we sweep the area. Burch assures us that no one has ever been stung on one of these walks.

We are nearing the end of “scorpion season” in late September, as the weather is getting too cold for the critters to wander freely. In winter, scorpions enter a low-activity state and hide in dark, quiet locations, like underground burrows, conserving their energy against the cold. I wonder if that means we won't see much—but then, after just a few minutes, a boy in my group begins to call out, “Scorpion, scorpion, scorpion!” Burch heads over, congratulatory as he crouches down a few inches away from the tiny, glowing arachnid. The rest of us follow, taking turns leaning closer to take in its luminous body, just a few inches long. It turns out that scorpions are perfectly happy to hang out under the UV light, surrounded by a large group of people taking photos. It doesn't move a single muscle as we pap it in awe.

Setting up kayaks at the edge of the Sand Hollow State Park reservoir, just as the moon begins to rise

Jaw-dropping colors appear in the sky over Sand Hollow State Park and its reservoir.

Ready for something a bit calmer, we make the early morning drive to our final destination: Hurricane, Utah—about 25 miles away from Zion National Park, and home to the lesser-known Sand Hollow. Initially, we had plans to do nocturnal canyoneering in Zion, but when the weather turned, it gave us permission to settle into a slower place.

For the past two years, Sand Hollow State Park—known for its large reservoir set against red-rock cliffs—has been offering a monthly full-moon paddle in the warmer months, to complement the hiking and biking trails that families enjoy year-round.

The paddle is only available from April through September (depending on the weather) and starts at moonrise—which is just after 7 p.m. for us, when our group meets ranger Callie Mallow on the shore of the reservoir. Just on the other side of sunset, the sky still has tinges of light to it, and we can hear the gentle chatter of dozens of other people who are still swimming, fishing, and hanging out on the water's edge as we pull our kayaks into the water. The park is open until 10 p.m., and those accompanied by a ranger can stay even later. I feel as though I'm at an after-hours summer camp, as I notice the people on piers, jumping in the water, and sitting at picnic tables nearby.

Two kayaks at the edge of Sand Hollow State Park reservoir at the end of the moonlit paddle

The night is relatively warm, as we make the gentle paddle out. We take pauses to float and listen to history; Mallow points at an island in the middle of the water: "a family of raccoons lives there", she tells us.

We let our kayaks rest when we near the isle, collecting among the rippling water to watch the moon continue its rise. I hear the water brushing against my kayak, and realize my eyes are now more used to navigating these wild spaces in the absence of the sun.

Our group is substantial. Sometimes there will 30 kayaks or paddleboards on the water at a time, Mallow says. “The moonlit paddle is pretty popular,” Mallow tells us from her own kayak, as we circle the island. "We hope programs like these both spark adventure and curiosity in visitors, and encourage neighbors to fall in love with their backyard.” As we navigate the water, we come across night fishers and a swimmer (“people train for the Ironman here,” says Mallow). I wonder if the scuba divers who were on the shore when we pushed off were entering or exiting. Before this trip, my imagination couldn't have fathomed so many ways to experience these lands after sunset.

As parks get increasingly crowded, I have to wonder if nighttime adventures will become a part of my regular routine. There are always new ways to discover America’s national and state park system—and now, I know that darkness can make even the most familiar places feel like a whole new world. Once you get past the fear of it—the dark, the height, the critters—you are free to soak up the feeling of being so small against the endless night sky, amazed by all the life that continues even once the sun has gone down.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

I Used NYT Cooking’s New AI-Powered Recipe Modifier, and It’s Actually Pretty Good

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

In an aside in the New York Times Cooking video I watched recently, where Melissa Clark made her lemony white bean soup with turkey and greens, the food reporter and recipe columnist invited viewers to leave a comment telling her what they do with the kale stems. It highlighted this sweet, funny relationship between a media brand’s personalities and its audience, a warm-casual vibe.

Below the article announcing the change on October 23, commenters loudly aired their grievances, threatening to cancel subscriptions, asking who wanted this, and how to turn it off.

After four days and 172 almost exclusively angry comments, commenting for the story was turned off.

My writing for WIRED over the past decade has shown me again and again that adding tech to a kitchen product is risky at best. I've repeatedly watched companies try and fail: Bluetooth connections for all the appliances, apps that lean too heavily on crummy recipes, toasters with optical sensors or touchscreens that still couldn't toast particularly well. Just about anything that was part of the "smart kitchen" tended not to be very smart at all.

Developing dependable, delicious recipes is hard, expensive work that can require a whole staff. Yet skimping can hurt the brand. Two notable companies with a deep bench—The New York Times Cooking and America's Test Kitchen—are cooking content makers. Recipes, which is to say delicious, high-quality, tested recipes, are what they do.

Since AI is often a lousy chef, giving it a role in the presentation of NYT's gold mine of content is a big risk.

Earlier this year, the Times allowed AI to help with a recipe-scaling feature (modifying ingredient quantities so "serves 4" turns into "serves 6") and converting from imperial measurements to metric—cups, tablespoons, and Fahrenheit to grams, milliliters, and Celsius. Now, in the iOS-only beta, a "customize this recipe" field appears just above the ingredient list.

"We put a lot of effort into trying to tell you when not to do something if it's no longer in the spirit of the recipe," says Camilla Velasquez, the general manager of NYT Cooking.

For its recommendations, the LLM looks at the recipe itself, the Times’ ingredient substitution guide, user comments, and then what it calls AI "drawing on our expertise," in that order. Velasquez says the AI should be a helper, not the main act, noting, "You shouldn't be chatting with it."

She explains that when the Times built the AI functionality, recipe editors helped create the original rule set, then evaluated how it worked at the end.

When I ask what this means for the jobs of 60 editors, writers, video journalists, and other employees—the team that creates the recipes people use and curates the vibe readers relate to—Velasquez stresses that NYT Cooking has been "hiring more than ever" and that it has "zero intention of publishing fewer recipes."

So far, she says people are mainly using it for ingredient and equipment substitutions, along with adjusting recipes for dietary needs. "Some people use the chatbot to scale, or they'll scale to match an ingredient," giving the example of someone who had only one cup of oil for a recipe that needed more.

As a food writer whose career has taken some substantial turns due to AI recently, I wouldn't say I've been clamoring for this, but I find the Times’ measured approach works pretty well.

I started testing it out by making chicken and dumplings using a five-star Alison Roman recipe with more than 6,000 ratings. I asked the AI tool to swap out bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs for boneless, skinless breasts. It thought about it for a moment—"simmering" in AI Cooking patois—and then offered up "Chicken Breast and Dumplings," with the "breast" in the title rendered in the red-orange color that indicates a modification. Just below the title, it said it was "Joseph's version made with AI. Not tested or endorsed by the original recipe's creators." Along with those visible changes, I could also toggle back and forth between my version and the original.

Once I started cooking, things went smoothly. I'm a recipe follower, not an improviser, and the Times’ recipes are easy to follow. The modifications were too. I was also impressed by how the app flagged that the chicken breast simmering time would be reduced because it cooks faster than thighs, and since they don't render as much fat as skin-on thighs do, it added some butter to the roux. These changes were incorporated into the right places in the recipe.

It all made sense except for one bit. It suggested the shredded, cooked breast go back into the pot for about another 30 minutes while the sauce thickens and the dumplings cook. I saw this coming and, well, I wasn't going to do that to my dinner. I put a handful of shredded meat in as directed so I could see what happened and reserved the rest. The shredded bits that were in for that extra half hour weren’t bad per se, but their texture was notably less succulent than what I'd reserved and way less than thighs would be.

This was the largest issue I encountered in all of my testing. If I had been the creator of the Times' original recipe, I'm not sure how I'd feel about the option to use a breast instead of thighs, which is not unheard of, but not very common in classic chicken and dumpling recipes. Perhaps it's something that could be fine-tuned or simply not offered as an option.

From there, it was fairly smooth sailing. I had it turn a Francis Lam roasted mushroom and broccoli grain bowl into a roasted mushroom, cauliflower, and squash grain bowl. The crucifer crisscross was an easy one-to-one, but I also told it to add a pound of delicata squash. It had me slice the squash into half moons so everything would finish at the same time, then upped the total amount of olive oil. Lam's poetic voice was preserved in both versions of the recipe, telling you to heat the olive oil "until it slips around the pan like water" before adding beaten eggs for festive dish-topping egg ribbons. My buddy Dave had come by for cribbage night, and this made for an enjoyable, healthy, pregame dinner before he went on to skunk me twice.

I'd been curious how it would handle batters, and it capably transformed Yewande Komolafe’s Toasted Sesame and Scallion Waffles into a yeasted version, which, unlike the original, gets an overnight in the fridge, letting the active dry yeast get bubbly and add an appealing tang.

A friend suggested Colu Henry's pasta and chickpea stew. I swapped canned diced tomatoes I had in my pantry for whole, and asked it to "add more vegetables." It gave options in line with the recipe—carrots, celery, onion, zucchini, mushrooms, and extra greens—then put what I chose into the right spots throughout the procedure so everything went in at the right time. It made for a healthy, hearty dinner.

Having been to the Portland Fermentation Festival recently and wanting guidance on incorporating a few extra ingredients, particularly garlic, into my sauerkraut, I used the AI to modify Lidey Heuck's original, saying "add carrots, garlic, and kale," which it suggested grating, thin slicing, and mincing, respectively, all in the right-feeling proportions. It also added more salt, since I was making more kraut. The big jar is sitting next to me as I type, and while it's several days from being ready, a sniff test tells me it's on its way to being a good batch.

Finally, I turned Sue Li's peanut noodles with spicy tofu into peanut udon with tofu, whitefish, and cucumber. I started by asking it to make the original less spicy, add veggies, and "keep the tofu," but add some good-looking rockfish I found at the grocery store. Impressively, since the fish was an add-on, it had me cook it, then set it aside and wipe out the pan (!) before cooking the tofu. Since I swapped udon for the original bucatini, it customized the noodle-cooking guidance, too.

It seems pretty natural that the Cooking section ran into some resistance when launching this feature. The NYT employs some of the best recipe creators in the business. Using AI to help us make substitutions is helpful, and the way the app integrates those changes into the recipes is impressive, but this tinkering with the original is essentially a subtractive process. How many of us are going to make substitutions and land on something better than Melissa Clark, Yewande Komolafe, or Francis Lam set out to make by design?

Instead, it's saving us a trip to the store, or helping us use something we have on hand, or making it gluten-free, serving as a good and rare example of how you can use technology to make a product incrementally better.

It also sounds like the commenters’ concerns have been heard. Velasquez let me know right away when we spoke that people will be able to hide the AI feature in the app if they want. The Times is also keeping the whole thing in iOS-only beta for now, and not saying when it would be available on other platforms.

That bond that you can see between the Cooking team and its users is a pretty sweet thing. Deep down, I'm guessing the upset Cooking commenters felt worried they were going to lose some of that human connection. I hope it never goes away.

We're heading into Xbox's 25th anniversary week! Xbox will officially be celebrating that milestone on Sunday, November 15th (exactly seven days from the time of posting this article), and we're hoping for some surprises along the way.

We already know of one surprise, which is that eight games will be joining Xbox Game Pass Essential on the 15th itself. Combine that with the six others during the rest of the week, and we've got over 10 brand-new additions heading to Game Pass during the course of the 25th anniversary period.

In case you missed them, those eight Game Pass Essential games on the 15th are as follows:

As for the rest, we've got three games arriving on the 10th, two arriving on the 11th, and one on the 12th. There's every chance Microsoft might add to these as we go, but these are the ones that have been confirmed so far.

Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Is It Too Late to Plant Amaryllis for Christmas? Here's How to Get Gorgeous Blooms in Time

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

Plant These 8 Hardy Vegetables in November – They’re Easy to Grow for Fresh Winter and Spring Harvests

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The clock is ticking, but there's still hope for a festive flower show. Choosing the right bulbs and growing conditions can make all the difference.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It’s not necessarily too late to plant amaryllis bulbs for Christmas, but timing does matter. Amaryllis typically blooms six to eight weeks after planting, so early to mid-November is your last chance to get bulbs in the pot if you want striking holiday flowers in time for the big day.

If you’re growing amaryllis for holiday flowers, choose the right type. Amaryllis usually refers to Hippeastrum, a South American native, not true Amaryllis, also known as belladonna lilies from South Africa. Most Hippeastrum bulbs sold this time of year are grown in the Southern Hemisphere and will bloom in six to eight weeks. Dutch-grown bulbs take longer, so avoid these.

Flowering also varies by the plant’s growing conditions, which means you have some control. While early or mid-November is the ideal time to plant a holiday amaryllis, you can do a few things to speed up the process if you’ve just missed the window.

Getting healthy holiday blooms isn’t just luck. How you choose and plant your bulbs is important. Start by selecting the best bulbs. Look for large, firm, and healthy bulbs without soft spots, damage, or mold. If you see a bulb that has the tip of a green stem emerging, grab it. This bulb has already begun to break dormancy and should flower sooner.

While unusual varieties can be expensive, you don't need to spend a lot of money on bulbs – Walmart sells preplanted bulbs for under $6.

Next, choose a good-quality potting mix, such as Miracle-Gro Indoor Potting Mix, available at Amazon, and a container with drainage holes that is only one or two inches (2.5 to 5 cm) wider than the bulb. Position the bulb in the pot so that the pointy end is up and one-third to one-half of the bulb is above the surface of the potting mix.

Water your planted bulb thoroughly, letting it all drain through, before setting it in a warm, bright spot. Keep the potting mix just slightly damp, never soggy. Amaryllis doesn’t need much water and can rot in soggy conditions.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

While you can’t significantly alter the timeline for flowering, a few easy tips encourage strong and steady growth:

You can’t do a lot to force it, but these conditions provide everything your plant needs for a healthy growth rate. Just don’t overdo it. Fertilizer, too much heat, and too much water can do more harm than good.

Be realistic about timing with amaryllis bulbs. If it’s late November, you might still have time for a Christmas flower. Choose the largest, healthiest bulb, with a bit of green growth, if possible, for the best start. Provide the best conditions and cross your fingers.

If it’s already December, you won’t get a flower by Christmas from a dormant bulb. However, you can probably find a potted amaryllis that has already broken dormancy and has a stem. This plant will likely bloom by Christmas.

You got some gorgeous blooms in time for Christmas, but now you want them to last. It’s time to switch your care strategy from encouraging growth to keeping flowers looking their best.

When the flowers do fade, snip them off to keep the plant looking tidy. Once all the flowers are done blooming, cut the stem back close to the bulb. With the right care, you should enjoy healthy, attractive flowers for two to three weeks.

Here are a few great options available to buy now, with beautiful blooms that will bring a festive splash of color to your home.

Whether you get flowers in time for Christmas or not, forcing an amaryllis bulb indoors is a great winter activity. Enjoy your blooms, even if they don’t open until January. And if you’re up for it, you can keep the plant going and encourage it to bloom again next year.

Mary Ellen Ellis has been gardening for over 20 years. With degrees in Chemistry and Biology, Mary Ellen's specialties are flowers, native plants, and herbs.

Wandrd’s Magnetic Camera Straps Are My New Favorite Accessory

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

More than a decade ago, Peak Design rethought the humble camera strap. This was a necessary development. When you buy a camera, manufacturers almost always toss in a chintzy, uninspired strap with their logo on it. These cheap straps have cheap mounting systems that make it hard to quickly remove the strap from the camera when you need to, like when you're attaching your camera to a tripod or gimbal and you’d rather not have a dangling strap whacking you in the face.

Peak Design solved this with its Anchor Links system, small circular nubs you leave attached to camera eyelets that make detaching or even swapping straps easy. The quick-connect mechanism is now the de facto industry standard and has inspired countless copycats.

Now, Wandrd has outdone that design using magnets.

“We're photographers ourselves, so we're constantly ideating, tinkering, thinking of ways that things can be better,” Ryan Cope, Wandrd cofounder, tells WIRED. “A camera strap like this has been at the back of our minds for a while.”

Wandrd—known for its camera bags—launched its Magnetic Camera Strap system on Thursday. It features a circular anchor similar to Peak Design, called “Torus Links.” The anchors attach to your camera’s eyelets and are rated to carry up to 198 pounds—so unless you're bench-pressing your camera, they'll do the job. If the cord does start to wear down over time, you'll first see yellow under the black fabric—a sign you should replace the Torus Link. If it's red, you should stop using the link immediately—a warning system borrowed from Peak Design. (Wandrd offers a two-year warranty.)

Unlike Peak Design's anchors, the Wandrd anchors look like donuts, with a hole in the middle. When you attach the strap, the embedded magnets guide the Torus Link into the strap's CoreLock mechanism, which pinches into the hole to secure the camera body. The magnets make connecting a strap easier, and they keep the strap fastened to the anchor, but you can’t just pull the strap off like you’re pulling a MagSafe accessory on your phone. You’ll need to use the strap’s dual-pinch mechanism to remove it from the Torus Link, a design that’s more secure.

The Torus Link is the donut-shaped object that attaches to your camera. On the right is the Magnetic CoreLock at the end of the strap. Pinch the top and bottom of this mechanism and let the magnets guide the strap into the Torus Link.

It’s a much faster way to connect and disconnect a strap, and that speed pays off in the field. This week, I had to photograph a product during a press briefing. Usually, I use Peak Design's Leash and, instead of taking off the strap, I bunch it up in my hand when it's not around my neck. It's not that it's hard to remove—it's just an extra step I have to think about. But Wandrd's magnetic system felt faster to unlock mid-shoot when I wanted to lower the camera for an awkward, low-angle shot, and reattaching the strap was practically instantaneous. I did the whole swap one-handed, with the magnets doing most of the work.

“I love magnets, man,” Cope says. “I love the simplicity they create—guiding things into place. You don't always have a place to put your camera down; I like that magnets help guide them and are a lot more secure, and they're also just fun.”

I have two Torus Links on my camera body, one on the right eyelet and another on the tripod baseplate. But my favorite configuration turns the strap into a single-point sling. The strap has a built-in “Torus Loop” on the buckle—clip one end directly into it to create a closed loop worn across your torso, leaving just a single magnetic connector to snap onto your camera. When you unclip the camera to shoot freely, the strap stays neatly in place across your body instead of sliding off your shoulder.

The nylon felt comfortable across my chest and near my neck—there's no padding, but that's intentional. Cope says Wandrd spent two years developing this custom “liquid nylon” tubular webbing that is softer along the edges, so it won't feel like the strap is cutting into your neck after a long day of wear. The lack of metal throughout the strap system also prevents the strap from scratching up your camera gear.

Best of all, you don't have to replace your strap to use Wandrd's system. If you have a strap you love—maybe a fancy leather strap someone gifted you—Wandrd is selling a DIY kit ($29) that lets you attach its Magnetic CoreLock system to the ends of your preferred strap. Otherwise, the strap kit comes in a 30-mm size ($69), a 15-mm “Lite" ($49), and a wrist strap ($39).

I don't know if Wandrd's system will see the same success as Peak Design's Anchor Links, but I don't see myself switching back anytime soon. Magnets are just too dang cool.

Doom delivers five minutes of terminal violence on an IBM mainframe

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Browser demo renders the classic shooter in ASCII, with no login required and considerable squinting encouraged

Can an IBM Z mainframe run Doom? Of course it can. The more interesting question is whether you can play it through a text terminal without ruining your eyesight.

WND Software's browser demo connects to an IBM Z mainframe over SSH using its terminal emulator. Wojciech Graj's DOOM-ASCII supplies the text-based carnage, replacing conventional graphics with colored characters. There's no sound.

It is, however, completely playable (although you might need to recalibrate your vision to work out what is going on, particularly with regard to distant objects). Sessions are limited to five minutes, with room for up to 50 simultaneous users, but we had no trouble getting in. ASCII also presented no issues once the game was in motion. This writer played the original shareware release on an Amstrad ALT386SX portable, so squinting at an indistinct demon felt reassuringly familiar.

That Doom can be persuaded to run on an IBM Z mainframe is not particularly noteworthy. The code has turned up everywhere from databases to PCB designers. Even the humble Pi Pico was roped into the fun.

Keeping the action playable through a text terminal is the more entertaining achievement. A year ago, an IBM consultant persuaded Doom to run on IBM Z and LinuxONE using VNC to bring the pixelated mayhem to life. Using a terminal emulator, while a neat demonstration of the capabilities of WND's software, is perhaps more an example of "because I can" masochism.

It's undeniably an enjoyable way to burn through five minutes. No installation or login required – just a willingness to shoot at punctuation. ®
