Polar bears are gathering close to this Canadian town—and co-existing with humans

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 10:17 AM EST

Polar bears (Ursus maritimus) are typically solitary creatures. But in a Canadian town on Hudson Bay, the predators gather along the coast every fall to wait for the ice to freeze.

“It’s an incredible phenomenon to experience,” Alysa McCall, director of science at Polar Bears International (PBI), tells Popular Science. “The bears return to this stretch of coast because it’s where the Bay freezes first, thanks to freshwater from the Churchill River plus the Bay’s currents and winds.”

Sea ice platforms help polar bears catch seals, which are their main source of prey. It’s usually harder for them to do so without the ice, as exemplified in a video PBI captured of a bear on the shore unsuccessfully trying to hunt a seal in the water.

This makes polar bears particularly vulnerable to climate change, which is bringing about longer ice-free seasons. In fact, polar bears in the Western Hudson Bay subpopulation today are stuck on land for around one month longer than their grandparents. What’s more, their numbers have approximately halved since 1979 because of sea ice loss.

Nicknamed the Polar Bear Capital of the World, the residents of Churchill, Manitoba have front-row seats to the annual polar bear gathering (you can also watch through their Polar Bear Live Cams). They are thus also exposed to conflict with these very large temporary neighbors, and climate change is extending this potentially dangerous overlap.

“When polar bears are on land, they are generally fasting. They sometimes venture into towns and communities to look for food, and human areas are full of things that attract curious bears – from trash bins to food smells,” McCall explains. “As climate change causes polar bears to spend more time on land, they have more opportunities to encounter people.”

As such, McCall and her colleagues at PBI have developed new tools to facilitate human-polar bear coexistence. One of them—cheekily named Bear-dar—is a medium-range polar bear detection radar currently being used at the Eureka Weather Station in Nunavut, Canada. Now the team is testing Bear-vision, a smart polar bear detection camera, at Churchill’s waste facility. According to McCall, it’s cheaper and simpler to use than Bear-dar, and works like a doorbell camera that warns you if there’s a polar bear hanging out outside.

“The team hopes to make the technology open-source so that Bear-vision will be widely available and accessible. More bear-safe tools mean fewer negative encounters, ultimately keeping people and polar bears separate and safer,” says McCall. “What’s happening along Hudson Bay shows how much communities can do to live harmoniously with nature.”

Here Is What an After-Dinner Walk Actually Does to Your Sleep, According to a Board-Certified Doctor

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Here’s a scenario many of us know too well: we eat a plate the size of a hubcap, then lose a fight with the couch by halftime. That slump has a name: postprandial somnolence, or what some people call a “food coma,” and it has a real biological basis.

As Outside previously reported, the smart move after a big dinner is a short, easy walk or “fart walk,” which helps food move through the digestive system—and may also help you get a good night’s rest.

I’m a board-certified internal medicine doctor; here’s how a walk, whether you decide to take a walk on your favorite trail or hit the treadmill, after a good meal may induce sleep.

Growing up, I was told that a big meal makes you sleepy because your stomach steals blood from your brain to digest the food. While digestion does pull extra blood to the gut, the brain guards its own supply closely, so blood flow isn’t the main reason you’re getting tired. Scientists now think a food coma results from several things piling up at once.

In lab studies, people got drowsier in the hours after eating, even when researchers accounted for the time of day and whatever was on the plate. Your internal clock dips in the early afternoon whether you eat or not, reducing performance. Sleep loss at night adds to the drowsiness, and so does a glass or two of wine. In experiments in mice, signals from a full stomach travel up the vagus nerve, a long nerve running from the brain to the gut, and switch on calming brain cells. Whether the same thing happens in people is still unknown.

What we do know is that some of the slump is out of your hands, but not all of it. How much you sleep, how much you drink, and what you do after you finish eating also matter. That’s where a walk can help.

The clearest evidence that walking after eating can be beneficial to digestion is seen in its effect on blood sugar. Getting moving right after a meal blunts the post-meal glucose spike better than sitting or exercising before you eat, and the sooner you start, the bigger the effect. Researchers think working muscles soak up sugar from the blood, and since most people sit after dinner, an evening walk may do the most good.

In healthy adults, eating more sugar and refined carbs has been tied to more nighttime stirring and lighter sleep. Among 18-year-olds, days with bigger glucose swings were followed by slightly shorter sleep. Adults who are overweight or those with obesity at risk for type 2 diabetes showed something similar: bigger daytime glucose swings were followed by earlier waking that night, and higher average glucose was tied to less efficient sleep.

Walking’s effects on sleep are promising but modest. A 2026 meta-analysis found that walking lengthened total sleep and cut time spent awake during the night, though it didn’t help people fall asleep faster, and it’s important to note that most trials tested daytime walking. A prevailing pattern was that sessions of 30 minutes or more showed clearer benefits for how well people felt they slept, so a longer walk might be the move.

A review article from the American Heart Association says that exercise is a zeitgeber, a cue that helps set the body clock. According to the review, physical activity generally improves sleep no matter what time of day you do it, and regular moderate-to-vigorous activity can strengthen the body’s daily rhythms.

The one wrinkle is timing. In a study of nearly 15,000 people wearing activity trackers, light or moderate exercise that wrapped up about two hours before bed wasn’t linked to worse sleep. Hard workouts late in the evening were. The easiest way to build in that buffer is to move dinner up. Eating earlier gives you room for the walk and leaves a longer gap before bed, which a large population study linked to better sleep quality.

Time your walk with sunset, and you may get a bonus: the fading light could help cue your body for sleep. As the sun goes down, the light reaching your eyes gets dimmer and less blue. Light-sensing cells in your eyes report that change to your brain’s master clock, which starts preparing to release melatonin and shift you toward sleep.

An after-dinner walk works best when it’s short, easy, and starts soon after you eat. Here are a few ways to get the most from it:

So the next time a food coma hits, lace up instead of lying down. A short, easy walk blunts the post-meal glucose spike, and as a regular habit, it may set you up for a better night’s rest.

The game's parody brands have taken over Miami.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With under two weeks to go until the release of Grand Theft Auto VI for PS5 and Xbox, Rockstar Games has been cranking up the marketing for what's already the most-anticipated video game of all time. The publisher has been rolling out prominent billboards, bus wraps and digital placements globally (including a hand-painted GTA VI mural that was controversially "ruined" in Brooklyn).

Most of the advertising blitz uses familiar key art showing the game's two playable protagonists: Lucia Caminos and Jason Duval. But some of the best GTA VI ads don't show the game at all. in Vice City itself (that is, Miami, Florida), Rockstar continues to have fun with its many fictional parody brands, and the ads show just what a huge cultural event GTA has become.

“Rockstar is toning down their humor.” 💀 Meanwhile, GTA 6 ads on real-life public kiosks in Miami: 💀😭 From “Go Topless” to “Suddenly You Are Big Enough,” Rockstar is bringing Vice City’s unhinged humor straight into the real world. The double entendres, fake brands, and savage satire are STILL HERE. 💀🔥 GTA 6 isn’t toning down the humor. Rockstar is putting it on public display. 😂 Via: @GTAVI_Countdown #GTA6 #GTAVI #GrandTheftAutoVI #RockstarGames #ViceCityOctober 26, 2026

To the disappointment of all those real brands that are GTA VI trendjacking, Rockstar doesn't do product placement, and it's also quite selective when it comes to real-world collaborations. Instead, the game will feature hundreds of parody brands.

Some of these have crossed over into real world advertising, with billboards and digital displays cropping up in Miami for the likes of Crowex watches, Beige shapewear and Dix makeup. The game's takeover of the Miami Heat's Kaseya Center has even included outlets for the fictional fastfood chains Burger Shot and Cluckin' Bell.

Many of the ads continue Rockstar's penchant for using innuendo as a sharp social satire on real advertising, cutting through to real messages that brands are often communicating albeit in more euphemistic terms. "Always come early," the watch brand Kronos declares. "Suddenly You Are Big Enough," proclaims jewellery brand Kowalski on an ad showing a diamond-heavy engagement ring.

All the GTA 6 ads I saw on a kiosk in Wynwood (Miami). These shuffled between real ads 😂 https://t.co/l46q91OHRZOctober 19, 2026

It's been estimated that Rockstar's total global promotional costs could reach up to $200 million or even $300 million over the game's full launch cycle. I've seen some people wondering how much adverts for fictional brands like Dix and Juice Loose can contribute to the cause if it's not clear what they're promoting (there's a small logo and QR code in the bottom of some of them).

But this is what makes them so ingenious. They avoid traditional game marketing fatigue by showing the setting rather than the product. GTA is known for its satire of American consumer culture, and fictional companies, ridiculous slogans, and parody advertising are a core part of its identity. Putting these brands out there communicates the game's tone without showing a single mission or gameplay mechanic.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

By blurring the line between marketing and world-building, the ads also create intrigue among newcomers. When people see an advert for a bizarre fictional product with no obvious explanation, it generates curiosity and conversation, which becomes the marketing.

But, ultimately, they also reward existing fans who recognise names like Ammu-Nation, Sprunk, eCola, or other in-universe brands. For long-timer players, these are inside jokes that make them feel like they're being invited back into a world they love.

An advert for a kids' cartoon called Macca & Friends reads "Coming November 19 on Weazel”. The real message is clear to anyone who knows Weazel as the in-universe media company since GTA IV. This kind of advertising doesn't just promote a product; it makes people feel like they're part of the phenomenon. They deepen immersion, keep people talking and cement the game's status as not just the most-anticipated video game of all time but perhaps the most hyped entertainment product in any category.

How frequently did Viking women fight in battle?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The Vikings are famous for being warriors, but how frequently did Viking women take part in battles?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Vikings were explorers, farmers, merchants and, most famously, warriors who launched raids across large parts of Europe.

Stereotypes of Vikings usually conjure up images of muscular men. But over the past few decades, researchers have turned their attention to women of the Viking Age (roughly A.D. 793 to 1066) and the roles they held. For instance, there are stories of "shield maidens" who fought alongside men, and some of these texts mention Viking women who fought in battle. The archaeological record also holds evidence, including graves of Viking Age women who were buried with war-grade weapons and shields.

It's still a matter of debate whether women who lived during the Viking Age could be warriors, but this discussion raises another question: It's one thing to have texts describing female fighters and archaeological records of women buried with weapons, but what can the evidence tell us about how frequently Viking women fought in battle?

The answer, experts told Live Science, largely depends on our interpretation of texts, artifacts, and burials of Viking Age women, which often face higher scrutiny than burials of men who were buried with similar (or even fewer) weapons.

The Vikings used a runic system of writing, but they left precious few texts behind. Instead, much of what we know about the Vikings was written about them by people from other cultures or by their Norse descendants.

One of the most evocative texts was written in the 12th century by the Danish historian and theologian Saxo Grammaticus. In "Gesta Danorum" (Latin for "History of the Danes" and translated by Jenny Jochens), he wrote that "there were once women in Denmark who dressed themselves to look like men and spent almost every minute cultivating [combat] skills." As "if they were forgetful of their true selves they put toughness before allure, aimed at conflicts instead of kisses, tasted blood, not lips, sought the clash of arms rather than the arm's embrace, fitted to weapons hands what should have been weaving, desired not the couch but the kill, and those they could have appeased with looks they attacked with lances."

Another text about the Vikings, the medieval Irish "Cogadh Gaedhelre Gallaibh" ("War of the Irish with the Foreigners"), refers to a woman known as the "Red Lady" leading a Viking fleet in a raid on Belfast in the 10th century.

Feed your curiosity with an exclusive mystery every week, solved with science and delivered direct to your inbox before it's seen anywhere else.

There's a lot of uncertainty, said Neil Price, an archaeology professor at Uppsala University in Sweden who has written extensively about the Vikings. The text in the "Cogadh Gaedhel re Gallaibh" that refers to the Red Lady is of debatable accuracy, Price told Live Science in an email.

He suspects that Viking women fought in battle but not frequently. In addition, "female warriors were probably a very small minority," Price said.

A modern-day reenactment depicting Viking warriors.

The account written by Saxo Grammaticus is also contentious. Thomas Kristian Heebøll-Holm, a history professor at the University of Southern Denmark, said Saxo Grammaticus is not a reliable source.

"Saxo Grammaticus does write about female warriors, but only in the parts of his work concerned [with] the legendary past of Denmark," Heebøll-Holm told Live Science in an email. "Furthermore, these writings are not accurate, but moral and fictitious," Heebøll-Holm said, noting that, in some instances, Saxo Grammaticus seemed to be drawing on the stories of the Amazons, a group of legendary female warriors whom the ancient Greeks believed in with stories about them dating back more than 2,500 years.

Archaeological evidence can also be challenging to interpret. Archaeologists know of about 30 Viking Age female burials containing weapons, including three with swords in Scandinavia. Even more Viking Age women's graves have items that could have been used as weapons ‪—‬ including spears, axes and knives ‪—‬ although these tools also could have been used for hunting or tending to farms. Either way, we can't be sure if these women fought in battle.

"The first concern is that the weapons were really used by the deceased and were not merely included as signs of special status or social rank acquired through inheritance," Deborah Shepherd, an independent researcher who holds a doctorate in archaeology and has studied the Vikings extensively, told Live Science in an email. She noted that graves are often not well preserved, making it difficult to gather information.

This 1889 illustration shows a grave at Birka in Sweden. It is now known that the person buried with war-grade weapons and riding gear is a woman.

There were likely "some few female warriors, but they were probably only found in the highest ranking families, and their warrior career was probably short-lived," Shepherd said. "They wouldn't have fought in the great armies of later centuries but possibly did fight in pre-Christian skirmishes." (The Vikings began gradually converting to Christianity between the ninth and 12th centuries.)

There is at least one known case of a Viking woman found with what may be combat-related injuries. A Viking woman found in Solør, Norway, had a head injury consistent with a sword strike. She was 18 or 19 at the time she died, was buried with weapons and lived around 900.

Ole Kastholm, a curator and senior researcher at the Roskilde Museum in Denmark, said there is a lot of uncertainty regarding how frequently Viking Age women fought in battle.

Weapons "do occur as a grave good in a handful of graves with female human remains," Kastholm told Live Science in an email. "Often it is just a single weapon [such] as a spear or [a] battle axe. But we know of at least one example of a female grave with a full set of weapons as well as riding equipment," which is located in Birka, Sweden.

"If this was a man's grave, researchers would have no doubt that it was a warrior grave, and this explanation must necessarily also apply when the interred is a woman," Kastholm said.

That said, there were female warriors in other cultures. "One can also note in passing that female warriors were an almost common feature of central Asian societies for at least a millennium before the Viking Age," Price wrote in his book, "The Sea-King and the Sorceress: Life and Death in the Viking Age" (Basic Books, 2026).

For instance the Scythians are a people who flourished in eastern Europe and Asia and Scythian women have been found buried with horses and weapons. In some cases, the Scythian women suffered combat wounds such as arrowheads and it's been speculated that they may have been an inspiration for stories of the Amazons.

See how much you know about ancient norsemen with our Viking quiz!

Owen Jarus is a regular contributor to Live Science who writes about archaeology and humans' past. He has also written for The Independent (UK), The Canadian Press (CP) and The Associated Press (AP), among others. Owen has a bachelor of arts degree from the University of Toronto and a journalism degree from Ryerson University.

This South Dakota Hunter Borrowed a Bow and Tagged a 197-Class Deer — His First Archery Buck

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

It wasn’t the best conditions for bowhunting in central South Dakota on November 3rd. It was misting and drizzling rain and the wind was blowing between 20 and 30 miles per hour.

But Colby Claussen had seen a huge buck the previous week out of a tree stand and decided to buy a ground blind and place it near the trail where the buck had walked.

“I spotted him a week earlier from my stand at nearly 70 yards walking a trail along a field edge near a standing corn field,” Claussen tells Outdoor Life. “He was working scrapes. I decided to buy an enclosed blind and put it in the woods 30 yards off the trail he walked.”

A week later he was in the blind hunting the buck he’d seen on trail cameras off and on for several years.

“I first saw him several years ago through binoculars while hunting,” Claussen says. “The buck was a good 10 pointer then and about 600 yards away – way too far to shoot.”

After that the 20-year-old hunter got trail camera pictures of the buck and watched him grow over several years.

“This year I saw him from my treestand working scrapes, but he was too far for me to try a shot with the bow I’d borrowed from a friend,” says Claussen, a just-graduated student who farms with his family. The giant buck would be his first ever taken with a bow.

By 1 p.m. on Nov. 3, Claussen was in his ground blind, protected from wind and rain. He spotted a couple small bucks and does that afternoon. Just 10 minutes before shooting light ended that day, he spotted the enormous whitetail he was after.

“He stepped out of the woods to work a scrape at 30 yards, but it was too thick to shoot, so I waited,” Claussen says. “The buck stayed on the trail and kept coming toward me until he got in an opening, broadside at 30 yards.”

Claussen drew his bow and shot an arrow tipped with a G5 Megameat 3-blade expandable broadhead. It hit the buck perfectly behind its shoulders, driving through both lungs, clipping its heart, and exiting the massive deer.

“I knew he was hit well, and he went to the treeline, walked in a small circle, and disappeared into the timber,” says Claussen, who owns Sodak Drone Application, an agricultural business. “I went to where he stood and found my arrow and plenty of blood.”

Claussen waited 30 minutes before trailing the deer, then quickly found the buck just 60 yards from where he’d shot it.

He called family members who soon arrived and helped with field dressing and loading the estimated 300-pound buck into a pickup truck. Then they took it to a meat processor, with the buck’s cape and head delivered to a taxidermist.

Read Next: The Best Hunting Blinds of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

A taxidermist measured Claussen’s buck at 198 1/8 inches, and another skilled deer rack scorer came up with 197 7/8 inches. The non-typical rack has nine points on one side, six on the other, with an inside spread of 21.25 inches. Antler base circumferences are 5 3/8 inches. The buck’s estimated age is 5.5 to 6.5 years.

“A couple years ago I had photos of that buck with half a broken rack from fighting,” Claussen says. “I quit hunting him when he broke his rack. But another hunter that year missed him in rifle season.

“I’m sure glad that hunter missed, and I got another chance to take my buck of a lifetime.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

Five examples of Mesoamerican modern architecture

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Mexican architects have been combining principles from ancient architecture with modern techniques. Curator and writer Su Wu picks five examples of this phenomenon from her Mesoamerican Modern book.

Wu’s book, Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes, details projects that illustrate this trend, from the 1970s to today, with a focus on residential architecture.

“These homes reflect the complex aesthetic cultures of Mesoamerica,” Wu told Dezeen. She chose the phrase that titles the book over “pre-Columbian” because she wanted to demonstrate that the monumentalism and logic of pre-Columbian Mexican architecture did not end with colonisation.

“I think these homes show instead that rather than being relegated to the past, these Mesoamerican materials, techniques, typologies, and forms are alive in the present and also perhaps inspiration or guides for the future.”

Each shows aspects of monumentalism that recall the stone architecture of the wider Mesoamerican culture, while acknowledging the diversity.

The book tries to depart from projects that just use symbols and focus on the logic of the buildings, noting specific characteristics such as patios as intrinsic the the design.

“For me, the most defining characteristics of Mesoamerican architecture are the ones that reflect underlying principles, rather than the disjunction when symbols become separated from their meaning,” Wu said.

“So throughout the book I wanted to highlight forms that arise from ideas such as the use of light or shadow as a physical object, or how homes are aligned to natural features, or the everyday presence of craft and the handmade, with an expanded sense of beauty as an attenuating consequence.”

Read on for five examples of the Mesoamerican modern style in Mexico with commentary by Wu.

“This 1973 home is one of a few 20th-century homes in Mesoamerican Modern, built by architect Agustín Hernández for his sister Amalia, the founder of Mexico City’s Ballet Folklorico.

“A poet as well as an architect, Hernández is celebrated for injecting ‘Mesoamerican semantics into the brute vocabulary of modernism’, but  wrote most of all about the geometric and cosmic principles at the heart of Mesoamerican architecture, and the ‘mathematical, almost symphonic rhythm’ of Mesoamerican structures and cities.

“His sister Amalia’s home was inspired by the convex forms of seashells as well as by the prominent ocular motifs in Mesoamerican imagery. “

Casa Mobius by Ernesto Gómez Gallardo, 1974

“Built by the architect for his own family in 1974, this house in Mexico City’s San Jeronimo neighborhood is today the studio and home of ceramicist Perla Valtierra.

“Shaped loosely like a mobius strip, with a triangular lattice allowing spaces to fold in upon themselves and a cantilevered living room, the home reconsiders both volumes and typologies, in rooms that are anything but square.

“The house is furnished with an eclectic mix of iconic Japanese, Brasilian and Mexican furniture, including a chair originally designed for the Museo Experimental El Eco in Mexico City by its founder Matthias Goeritz, a close friend and frequent collaborator of Luis Barragan.”

“Architect Teodoro González de León once described the patio as a defining characteristic of a Mesoamerican home, a space that is at once enclosed and exterior, and open to the elements.

“In this house in Estado de Mexico, Mexico City-based architect Fernanda Canales reclaimed land that had been devastated by cattle overgrazing, creating an interlocking series of patios that, in their deliberately undefined sense of function, create more space without having to build more of it.

“Each room in the home, which is approached through a pyramidal foyer with an opening to the sky, looks out onto courtyards or landscape, replete again with plants of the Americas.”

Casa Cometa by Taller Mauricio Rocha + Gabriela Carrillo, 2019

“In this home inspired by the spatial organization of ceremonial Mesoamerican sites, communal areas extend horizontally, while private spaces descend and rise vertically, like a dramatic bedroom tower with a daily view of both the sunrise and the sunset over the Pacific.

“With its overlapping platforms and passageways, the space designed by Gabriela Carrillo and Mauricio Rocha reflects the multiple planes of existence in Mesoamerican cosmology.

“In this house, a walkway weaves under the swimming pool, which itself slips under a long orthogonal platform, cut through with a descending staircase.”

“Casa Monte is a one-bedroom house with no exterior doors. Instead, the house deliberately discourages approach by appearing like a fortress over the tree line, and by its placement on a stepped platform, which creates a sense of separation from the jungle floor below.

“The open space with a pool cutting across the interior suggests the idea that architecture exists foremost as a way to engage with nature.

“Cast by Matos himself in earth-tinted cement on the beach where the house now sits, the home’s hundreds of individual forms were fitted together on-site.”

Feature: Box Art Brawl: The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Ultimate Showdown

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Welcome all, to a very special edition of Box Art Brawl!

Looking back at last week, we checked out Revenge of the 'Gator to celebrate its addition to Nintendo Switch Online. Both designs for this one are great in their own way, but it was Japan that took home the trophy with 58% of the vote, leaving North America and Europe to mop up the remaining 42%.

This week, we're doing something special. We've already covered the original Zelda: Ocarina of Time for the N64 along with the 2011 3DS remake. However, with the release of the Switch 2 remake, we thought we'd throw every box art into the ring for an ultimate showdown.

You'll note that we haven't included every Ocarina of Time release here. The bonus disc included with Wind Waker pre-orders for the GameCube also included the logo for Master Quest, so we're not adding it here. Similarly, the Collector's Edition disc for GameCube had no direct reference to Ocarina of Time on its box art, so it too has been excluded.

So with that said, let's get cracking, hm?

Review The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time (Switch 2) - A Stunning Remake, But Not Quite Definitive

Round Up The Reviews Are In For The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time On Switch 2

Feature Song Of Time - Every Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Release, From N64 To Switch 2

This is the classic box art, right? The one of which most of you will likely be familiar. It's simple yet effective, with the game's iconic logo presented against a plain gold background. Chef's kiss. Did it really need anything else? Probably not, and it served as a wonderful thematic continuation of A Link to the Past.

Europe's design is equally simple, yet equally stunning. This time, we have a jet black background punctuated by a shining gold logo in the centre. In fact, all of the text here is in gold. Again, it's the simplicity that really sells it, and the contrast to the North American version is particularly striking.

Japan's N64 approach is very cool. It utilises the portrait orientation to great effect, showcasing an image of Link riding Epona, while flames surround the outside of the composition. There's a bit more going on here compared to the western designs, but there's still a sense of simplicity with the approach. It's not busy at all, and we love the contrast of the black and white background against the coloured logo.

The North American approach for the 3DS takes the same image of Link and Epona from the original Japanese release. It also leans heavily on that classic gold presentation, resulting in a classy yet eye-catching final image.

This here is the winner of our Ocarina of Time 3D brawl, and it's easy to see why. We can see Link and Epona depicted over on the right, but your eyes are just immediately drawn to the stunning landscape of Hyrule Field. Lon Lon Ranch, Hyrule Castle, and Death Mountain are all visible, and it's just such a stunning interpretation of the game's most iconic environments.

Okay, finally we have the box art for the latest release on Switch 2, and holy moly, it's absolutely gorgeous. Many folks will have picked up the textured foil version, and this just adds a whole new layer to its beauty. Interestingly, this is the only box art to feature Child Link, but it's a nice approach. It really delivers that sense of whimsy and adventure you feel during those opening portions of the game, and it's just such an impactful composition. Lovely stuff. Changing the logo text to gold is a nice touch too.

Sheffield United man answers question over long-term future after West Ham, QPR summer interest

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Sheffield United saw Harrison Burrows endure a tougher 2025/26 season after a fantastic debut campaign in 2024/25. Rival Championship clubs were lurking in the summer in case SUFC opted to cash in on the 24-year-old, with West Ham and QPR credited with interest while Burnley saw a late bid rejected.

However, Burrows has been back in the side and back in form in recent weeks. While speaking to The Star, the former Peterborough United man was asked about his long-term future and whether he sees it with the Blades, to which he responded: “Yeah, 100%. I’m really enjoying my football here. I really enjoy being with the lads, I really enjoy the club and I really enjoy the city. So, yeah, I’m very happy.”

Burrows is currently under contract at Bramall Lane until 2028. Sheffield United are looking to tie down a number of players to new deals, and the left-back is one of them alongside Sydie Peck, Femi Seriki and Michael Cooper.

As touched on before, Burrows endured a fantastic debut year at Sheffield United. After signing from Peterborough United, he stepped up to the Championship brilliantly as he recorded six goals and six assists in 48 games for a Blades side that made the play-off final.

Form proved harder to come by last term, and Sam McCallum emerged as the first choice at left-back before injury hit. He locked down his spot in Chris Wilder’s side early on this season too, performing well while Burrows was sidelined himself.

Harrison Burrows’ Sheffield United stats (all competitions)

However, injury has cast McCallum to the treatment room once again, and Burrows has made a fine return to the team. He has started the last five Championship matches, with only Saturday’s meeting at Charlton Athletic ending in defeat.

Burrows’ form has served as a reminder of what he offers on the left, with his technical ability making him a creative asset for SUFC. There are questions over the defensive side of his game at times, but the quality he offers with the ball can see him play a highly influential role when he’s in good form.

Aged 24, Burrows is already approaching 300 career games. He played 185 times for former club Peterborough United, and Saturday’s start against Charlton Athletic marked his 101st appearance for Sheffield United.

It means he has both an excellent amount of experience and plenty of room to keep developing. Burrows spent his youth in midfield and broke into first-team football in this position too, so he still has progress to make defensively.

With improvements in this area, SUFC will have a well-rounded left-back with strong experience and leadership abilities – as shown by his past captaincy at Peterborough United.

A challenging season and summer interest brought questions over his Bramall Lane future, but Burrows can be a valuable asset in the now and future if a new long-term deal is agreed.

Nintendo's Bill Trinen Explains Why Ocarina Of Time Is The Perfect Place To Start In The Zelda Universe

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

If you're in a position where you've never actually played a Zelda game before, Nintendo's Bill Trinen just wants to let you know Ocarina of Time is the "perfect place" to start.

Although this comment just happens to line up with the arrival of the Switch 2 remake, Nintendo's VP of Player & Product Experience really is convinced this is the best game to begin with, as it's a "foundational story", where the player is taught everything they need to know about the world and characters.

Here's what he had to say at the remake's launch event in San Francisco this week:

Bill Trinen: "This is the perfect place for your first Zelda game, because you are gonna learn everything that there is about the world and the characters that will serve sort of as the foundation for all the others, all the games that came after.

"This game is sort of a really foundational story for the lore of the Legend of Zelda. It really tells the story of Link and his journey becoming a hero. It tells the story of Link and his connection to Zelda and their relationship with Ganondorf. And overall, it communicates kind of what is at the moral core of is all the series, which is the idea that wisdom and courage combined can overcome power."

There will likely be more eyes than ever on the Zelda series in 2027, when the live-action film arrives in cinemas in April. So Ocarina of Time truly is a fantastic entry point into the Zelda universe if you haven't played the games before. The remake on Switch 2 also updates the gameplay and mechanics to offer a more accessible experience.

Review The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time (Switch 2) - A Stunning Remake, But Not Quite Definitive

News Nintendo Excited About Broadening Zelda's Audience With Live-Action Movie

"That's one of the most exciting things for us"

What are your thoughts about Bill's comments here? In terms of Ocarina's story and universe, do you think it's the perfect entry point into the Zelda series, or would you recommend starting with something else? Let us know in the comments.

Liam is a news writer and reviewer on Nintendo Life. He's been writing about games for more than 15 years and is a lifelong fan of many iconic video game characters.

I mean it's a prequel that provides the framework for every game that comes after it, it's one of the earliest games in the series, it's pretty much the only 3D game that doesn't really have one massive unique quirk that dominates the game's structure and is just pick-up-and-play, and the only games set before it are two highly experimental side things that are still heavily rooted in the plots of other games, it'd be weird if it wasn't the best entry point at least on the 3D side.

2D though I'd probably say the best place to start is the Link's Awakening remake? It's not my favorite 2D one - Link Between Worlds is - but Link's Awakening is where a lot of the series structure first really came into being, I think it's a more tightly designed experience than LttP is (not sure which I actually prefer but LttP definitely has some early growing pains with some of its choices), and the Switch version has some handy conveniences like keeping the sword equipped that the GB version lacks.

And IS what Zelda games are supposed to be.

Everyone wants an electric pickup truck. I want more electric 125cc motorcycles

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

If you follow the EV industry in North America, you’d be forgiven for thinking that the future of transportation revolves around electric SUVs and pickups. Every week seems to bring another teaser from a startup promising to reinvent the family car or truck. The latest electric SUVs, “coming soon” tiny trucks, lifestyle trucks, modular trucks, etc. Companies like Slate, TELO, and countless others have generated plenty of excitement, even if many of them still have everything left to prove.

Don’t get me wrong – I hope they succeed. We need more affordable electric vehicles. But after spending nearly two decades covering a specific part of the electric-vehicle space and a good chunk of that time traveling around the world to see how people actually get around, I can’t help but wonder if we’re chasing the wrong prize here.

Because while the West keeps trying to electrify pickup trucks and SUVs, much of the world has already settled on a far more sensible transportation appliance.

Western readers often think of a sedan as the world’s “normal” or “sensible” vehicle.

But go traveling almost anywhere in Asia, and that illusion disappears in about five minutes.

The roads are filled with Honda Clicks and CG125s, Hero Splendors, Yamaha NMAXs and YBR125s, and countless other small-displacement motorcycles. They carry workers to jobs, students to school, families to dinner, and every imaginable cargo in between.

They’re the delivery vans. They’re the commuter cars. They’re grocery getters, family transportation, and work trucks. And perhaps more importantly, they transport hundreds of millions of people every single day.

You don’t have to spend long in places like the Philippines before realizing something else: these aren’t really “motorcycle cultures.” They’re just transportation cultures. People ride these machines because they make sense.

And I’ve done it myself to see what all the fuss was about. Sure, I’m an e-motorcycle guy through and through. But I’ve been there to ride a mile in their motorcycle sandals, and so I get it.

Even so, having had one cheek on each saddle, I still have to think there’s a better way than the tried-and-true 125cc combustion engine motorcycles that have become the workhorse of much of the world.

The extreme proliferation of small motorcycles is what makes the current electric motorcycle market feel so strange. If there were ever a class of gasoline vehicle that batteries are already capable of replacing, it’s the 125cc combustion engine motorcycle.

These bikes aren’t demanding. They generally produce a modest 10-15 hp (8–11 kW) of power. They cruise comfortably around 45-60 mph (70-100 km/h). Most riders travel relatively short daily distances measured in dozens of miles, not hundreds.

A modern electric motorcycle with a 4-6 kWh battery can comfortably accomplish those same tasks while offering smoother acceleration, quieter operation, lower maintenance, and dramatically lower operating costs.

Unlike replacing a heavy-duty pickup truck that tows livestock trailers across Montana, replacing a 125cc commuter bike is already well within today’s battery technology. In many ways, it’s one of the easiest transportation sectors to electrify.

Instead, North America’s electric motorcycle market developed in an odd direction.

We have electric bicycles, and those are great for urban commuters. But then we jump almost straight to premium electric motorcycles costing $15,000 to $25,000. The practical middle has remained surprisingly sparse.

The ONYX RCR became something of a cult favorite by proving there was genuine demand for lightweight, motorcycle-like electric transportation without superbike performance.

Ryvid’s Anthem pushed further, adding highway capability while still remaining approachable and relatively affordable.

The new LiveWire S4 Honcho also feels like an encouraging step toward a lighter, more practical electric motorcycle instead of another oversized premium machine.

Even Infinite Machine’s Olto, while occupying a slightly different category, demonstrates growing interest in practical urban electric two-wheelers that prioritize transportation over headline horsepower.

These companies are proving something important: People don’t necessarily need 100 horsepower. Many simply need transportation.

Don’t misunderstand me. Electric pickup trucks absolutely have their place. Electric SUVs do too.

But we’ve spent the last decade pouring enormous engineering effort into electrifying some of the least energy-efficient passenger vehicles on the road.

Meanwhile, we’ve largely ignored one of the most efficient gasoline vehicle classes humanity has ever created.

A typical 125cc combustion engine motorcycle can already achieve fuel economy approaching or exceeding 100 mpg (2.35 liters/100 km). An electric equivalent becomes almost absurdly efficient.

We’re talking roughly 4-6 kWh per 100 kilometers (62 miles) – just a fraction of the energy consumed even by many compact electric cars. I once did the math, and a light electric motorcycle can travel 10,000 km (roughly 6,000 miles) for around $160 in electricity. Basically, your electrical bill doesn’t even notice when you own an electric motorcycle. Charging it simply doesn’t move the needle.

Instead of asking how we can fit ever-larger battery packs into increasingly larger SUVs and trucks, perhaps we should also be asking how many trips don’t need a large vehicle in the first place.

Transportation statistics across much of the developed world tell a familiar story.

Most trips involve one person. Maybe there’s also a backpack in the mix. Maybe there are some groceries getting picked up on the way home. Occasionally, there’s another passenger.

Those things are exactly what small motorcycles have been doing around the world for decades.

I recently spent some time in the Philippines, where I watched motorcycles carrying impossible-looking loads every day. Entire families would ride together. Construction materials. Furniture. Livestock. I saw two pigs on a 125cc bike, I kid you not. I’ve seen even crazier things in Thailand.

Again, I’m certainly not advocating that we adopt some of those practices in the West – there are obvious safety considerations – but it demonstrates just how incredibly capable these machines can be.

Even within normal Western expectations, a 125cc-class motorcycle is capable of handling an astonishing percentage of daily transportation needs.

This is where discussions about motorcycles often become unnecessarily polarized.

No, motorcycles aren’t ideal in every situation. Yes, heavy rain exists. Snow exists. Families with multiple children exist. Long interstate road trips exist.

Nobody is suggesting a 125cc electric motorcycle should replace every car.

If a household can replace even half of its daily driving with a lightweight electric motorcycle, that’s already a massive reduction in energy consumption, congestion, parking demand, and transportation costs.

Transportation doesn’t have to be all-or-nothing. So enough with this “but what if it rains?” stuff. Either put on a jacket that day or drive your car. But for the majority of the trips each year you take where it’s not raining, you could have done it in a much more efficient (and fun) way.

One of the biggest conversations surrounding EV adoption is charging. But smaller electric motorcycles barely participate in that problem. A 4-5 kWh battery charges overnight from an ordinary household outlet. The outlet you plug your laptop charger into is basically the equivalent of a Level 2 public charger for an e-motorcycle – that’s how fast it is.

DC fast charging isn’t really necessary on a light electric motorcycle. There’s no need for an expensive home charger. There’s no massive battery pack. Just plug it in before bed and wake up with a battery that’s been fully charged and waiting on you for hours already.

And if you want to go wild, there are swappable battery networks like Gogoro in Taiwan or Vammo in South America that can make charging unnecessary.

Another advantage rarely discussed is intimidation. Many people don’t want a 600 lb (270 kg) motorcycle producing superbike acceleration. They want something approachable, something lightweight, maneuverable, easy to park, and easy to live with.

That’s exactly why 125cc motorcycles became so successful globally.

Electric versions preserve all of those advantages while making them even easier to ride thanks to instant torque and the absence of clutch work. Many of them even come with reverse mode, making them easier to park and wiggle around even on hilly terrain.

One reason Europe has seen more progress here is that the regulatory framework already exists.

Manufacturers regularly market bikes as “125cc equivalents,” and consumers immediately understand what that means.

Licensing systems and insurance companies recognize the category. It’s a mature transportation class.

North America, by comparison, often treats motorcycles as either recreational toys or large-displacement touring machines. We’ve largely forgotten that much of the world simply sees them as transportation.

Ironically, younger riders may already be showing us the way forward.

The explosion in popularity of Sur Ron-style electric motorcycles and lightweight e-motos demonstrates that younger generations are perfectly willing to embrace two-wheeled electric transportation.

Not everyone buying those bikes is looking for motocross. Many simply enjoy the freedom, accessibility, affordability, and fun that come with traveling on two wheels.

Rather than dismissing that trend, perhaps we should recognize it as an opportunity. Today’s recreational rider often becomes tomorrow’s commuter.

If someone discovers they enjoy getting around on an electric two-wheeler, moving from a lightweight e-moto to a practical 125cc-equivalent commuter becomes a much smaller leap than convincing someone to abandon their car entirely.

One observation has stuck with me after so many years of covering the electric vehicle industry and traveling through countries where motorcycles dominate daily life. The West has spent years trying to electrify the largest, heaviest, and least efficient vehicles on our roads. Meanwhile, we’ve paid surprisingly little attention to electrifying the vehicle that already moves much of humanity every single day.

At least in the US, scooters and light motorcycles for transportation are considered things poor people ride. They're not good for upselling options, they don't have a big margin per unit, so they don't really fit into the usual channels for making and selling transportation stuff. And US roads outside urban (and a few suburban areas) are pretty much designed to kill people who ride small two-wheel vehicles.

There are folks who will figure out ways to distribute and sell these things in quantity -- perhaps starting with local bike stores -- and start eating the revenue of the bigger motorcycle and car folks. One of the things that's kept bikes and scooters/small cycles apart in the past is the noise and grime of the internal combustion engines, so you need separate shops and selling areas for them. Electrification changes that.

I’m not arguing that everyone should ride a motorcycle. And I’m certainly not arguing that cars no longer have an important place.

But perhaps we’ve underestimated just how many people could benefit from something in between.

Not just an electric bicycle, and not just a $25,000 electric motorcycle. Just an honest, practical, affordable electric equivalent of the vehicle that already keeps much of the world moving.

Because if we’re serious about accelerating electric transportation, maybe the future isn’t another electric pickup truck. Maybe it’s finally giving the world’s favorite motorcycle an electric motor.

Internet governance about to get its quadrennial stress test at ITU meeting

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

278 proposals on table, and some fear they might shift power to governments

The International Telecommunications Union (ITU) kicks off its quadrennial plenipotentiary on Monday, and internet governance organizations are bracing in case the event becomes a forum for nation-states to try to challenge their roles.

The ITU is the United Nations' specialized agency for information and communication technologies, meaning its members are nation-states. The body's role is to facilitate international connectivity, which it does by managing radio spectrum, satellite orbits, and working on relevant technical standards.

Its key standards are pervasive. ITU-T X.509 sets out the rules of public key infrastructure. ITU-T E.164 governs telephone numbers. ITU-T H.264 and 265 define the video codecs used for most online streams.

The ITU's role is largely uncontested, yet its work gets very close to that of the organizations who work on technologies that make the internet work.

Those bodies include the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF); the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN), which oversees the domain name system; and the regional internet registries (RIRs), which manage IP addresses. The World Wide Web Consortium (W3C) works on HTML, CSS, and accessibility.

Governments participate in work at those internet governance bodies but are just one voice among many and can't override the consensus-driven decision-making process they use, under a system which has come to be known as the multi-stakeholder governance model.

At a 2025 event called WSIS+20, the United Nations endorsed multi-stakeholder governance by deciding that its Internet Governance Forum (IGF) should become a permanent body. That decision effectively endorsed multi-stakeholder governance, and kept the ITU out of internet standards.

Yet the IGF hasn't met since WSIS+20, so it is yet to start operating meaningfully as a permanent body.

This year's ITU meeting is therefore a moment at which stakeholders can stress-test the delicate balance between the ITU, internet governance bodies, and the emerging IGF. Such a test could see ITU members try to have it pursue new fields that overlap with work performed by internet governance organizations.

This has already happened before. China tried to have the ITU work on "New IP" – a major revision to internet protocol, a technology the IETF manages.

In 2022, Russia and China backed a candidate for the ITU presidency who favored giving nations a stronger role in internet governance, potentially by having the ITU take over some standards work. That candidate was not elected.

Ahead of next week's meetings, the Internet Society published a background paper that declared it is "actively challenging proposals that could affect the Internet's underlying technical architecture or increase fragmentation, as well as those that could affect connectivity, particularly for remote, underserved, and unserved communities."

The society is watching for resolutions on AI, quantum technologies, and other emerging areas that could embed specific technologies in ITU mandates. It also observes that decisions are yet to be made on which bodies should oversee governance of low-earth orbit satellite constellations, submarine cable resilience, and spectrum policy.

"ICANN welcomes the recognition of the consensus-based WSIS+20 outcomes," the organization told The Register by email.

"At the same time, proposals that seek to expand the ITU's role in areas such as IP-based networks, the Domain Name System, management of Internet resources, and Internet-related public policy issues raise concerns about overlapping mandates and the creation of parallel processes that could affect the health of the Internet."

ICANN didn't point to any specific proposals that raise such concerns, and at ITU meetings most proposals – of which there are 278 this time around – are decided on consensus.

It is, however, possible for ITU members to amend proposals during meetings, leaving open the possibility of significant policy changes coming up for a vote during the 18 days the event occupies. ®

Premier League club tracking Middlesbrough star Will Lankshear amid historic form

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Middlesbrough only signed Will Lankshear in the summer, but the striker’s stunning form has brought excitement over his next steps. England boss Thomas Tuchel has eyes on the Boro star, and now, The Sun states Newcastle United are closely watching his performances.

Scouts were set to watch Lankshear in action against Wrexham on Wednesday, but injury kept him sidelined for that game and the 0-0 draw at Watford on Saturday. Kim Hellberg‘s side failed to score in both games, highlighting how much they are missing their star striker.

Lankshear has 13 goals in 13 Championship games, and 15 goals in 16 matches for the Teesside outfit across all competitions. No Boro player has started a season in such prolific form since club record goalscorer George Camsell in 1926.

Lankshear has been viewed as an exciting prospect for some time. Spurs snapped him up from the Sheffield United academy at a youth age and he really announced himself on the senior stage on loan at Oxford United, where he scored 12 goals and provided three assists in 47 games across the 2025/26 season.

Despite such success and encouragement over his future, there were eyebrows raised when Middlesbrough struck a deal worth £20m with add-ons to sign him in the summer. However, Lankshear has already justified the hefty fee.

Lankshear has scored four Championship braces on his way to the top of the goalscoring charts. He was on a run of seven goals in five games before injury hit and ruled him out of Boro’s last two matches.

The 21-year-old’s lethal finishing and trajectory have drawn early comparisons to England captain and Ballon d’Or winner Harry Kane. Lankshear is currently averaging a goal every 78 minutes for Middlesbrough, so it is little surprise to see the England national team and Premier League club Newcastle United tracking his performances.

They will not be the only ones with eyes on Lankshear, who is viewed as one of the country’s most promising young attackers.

Lankshear only moved in the summer and is under contract until 2031. It means Boro aren’t under pressure to cash in.

However, to keep him long term, if Middlesbrough are to have a chance of fending off top interest, they have to ensure their trajectory matches that of Lankshear. The only way to do so is by winning promotion to the Premier League this season.

If not, there will be no shortage of clubs ready to spend big on bringing him in. Newcastle United are the only concretely reported admirer at present, but he is sure to have alerted more sides.

Middlesbrough sit 2nd in the Championship table, despite failing to win either of their last two games.

STAT+: Why the latest heart drug failures should make us all more skeptical of AI hype

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Senior Writer, Medicine, Editorial Director of Events

Matthew Herper covers medical innovation — both its promise and its perils.

The next time someone tells you that artificial intelligence, or any other new technology, is going to cure every disease, point them to the effort to fight heart attacks and strokes, which together are the largest killer in the developed world.

Because again and again, pharmaceutical researchers have learned the same hard lesson: Technologies that were supposed to speed new medicines to patients have fallen short. Two studies presented Saturday at the annual meeting of the American Heart Association are a perfect example. A new understanding of genetics was supposed to increase the odds of success — and did anything but.

One study showed that a Novartis drug, pelacarsen, had no effect on the rate of heart attacks and strokes despite lowering a substance in the blood called Lp(a), which genetics said should reduce cardiovascular risk. Another large study, from Novo Nordisk, of an anti-inflammatory targeting a protein called IL-6 also showed no benefit.

6 Gardening Jobs You Must Finish Before the First Hard Freeze – Or You’ll Regret it in Spring

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

What Your Hydrangeas Need in November – 5 Ways to Get Plants Winter-Ready and Kickstart Your Best Blooms Ever

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

7 Household Trash Items You Should Save in Fall to Protect Plants From Frost

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A hard freeze can do damage you won't see until spring, from cracked pots to rotten bulbs. These tasks can't wait for winter – and the window is closing fast, so hurry!

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Even in November, backyards can look like they got some time left, perhaps with a few late-season tomatoes still clinging to the vine and container tropicals green on the patio. But all it takes is one hard freeze, dropping overnight temperatures with a day or two’s notice. Suddenly, tender leaves turn black and crops are ruined. What’s worse is that much of the damage stays hidden until spring.

You’ll only discover a split copper pipe when you turn on the hose in April, or realize you lost your prized dahlia tubers when green shoots fail to emerge. Sure, not every chore needs to be tackled now. Routine pruning and bed edging can wait until late winter. However, certain tasks carry a hard deadline set by the weather: this includes anything involving overwintering plants, as well as plumbing fixtures that hold water.

In some yards, this deadline has already passed, and in others, it's coming up this week. Completing the following tasks before temperatures plunge can save you hundreds of dollars in expensive winter damage, and keeps everything in the best shape for spring. Here’s how to make sure your garden emerges strong and healthy next year.

Understanding the difference between a light frost and a hard freeze can give you the lead time you need. Growers sometimes talk about frosts and freezes interchangeably, but the differences matter. A light frost occurs when air temperatures hover right at the freezing threshold (32°F / 0°C), forming ice crystals on leaf surfaces. While tender annuals like basil and coleus blacken quickly at this temperature, cold-tolerant vegetables like kale shrug it off (and even taste better).

A hard freeze is more aggressive. It occurs when ambient temperatures drop to 28°F (-2°C) or lower and remain there for several hours. At this depth of cold, ice crystals form inside plant cell walls, bursting cellular membranes and killing unhardened woody stems. A hard freeze also freezes water inside pipes, outdoor faucets, and irrigation lines. True frozen ground, where soil hardens into an impenetrable crust, usually develops weeks later after sustained sub-freezing weather.

Northern gardens are often done by November, while milder regions may still have several weeks. Because first-freeze dates are geographical moving targets, your regional location dictates your timeline. Gardeners in northern USDA hardiness zones 3-5 often face hard freezes by mid-October, while those in milder zones 7-9 may not see temperatures dip below 28°F until late December or January. This can help prevent panicking too early – or waiting too late.

Don’t wait for a freeze warning to assemble your gear. Keeping insulated spigot covers, garden forks, frost fabric, and storage crates on hand now ensures you can secure your yard quickly when the first blue whistler rolls in. To help you track things, it’s also a good idea to have a thermometer on standby. The TempPro TempAir 500 FT Indoor/Outdoor Thermometer, available from Amazon, can help you to stay one step ahead of any overnight shocks. Then you’re ready to tackle these tasks…

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

One frost can ruin tomatoes, peppers, eggplants, and basil. Even near-freezing temperatures in the mid-30s can cause chilling injury to peppers and eggplants, dulling skin quality and introducing rot spots. So strip your vines of all mature crops before freezing weather arrives. Use sharp scissors or snips like Fiskars Micro-Tip Garden Snips from Amazon.

Gather every ripe tomato, along with full-sized green tomatoes that have developed a glossy, pale-green sheen. Placed in a cardboard box indoors at room temperature, mature green fruits will ripen off the vine over the next few weeks. Leave small, hard fruits behind, as they lack the maturity to ripen. Pull up basil plants to process into pesto or dry indoors.

However, you can leave cool-season brassicas and root crops where they are. Collards, Brussels sprouts, kale, carrots, and parsnips tolerate temperatures well below 28°F (-2°C). The cold triggers these hardy crops to convert complex starches into plant sugars, which can help them taste sweeter after frost.

In regions with cold winters (typically zones 7 and colder), tender summer-flowering bulbs, tubers, and corms won’t survive in freezing ground. Leaving dahlias, cannas, calla lilies, caladiums, gladioli, and tuberous begonias in the soil too long, and the fleshy storage organs will freeze, decompose, and turn to mush. Dahlias and cannas benefit from waiting until an initial light frost blackens the foliage. (Tuberous begonias are more tender and often need to get lifted earlier as the leaves yellow.)

Once that light freeze hits, don’t wait for the ground to freeze solid. Cut black stems back to 4-6 inches (10-15cm). Carefully insert a garden fork into the earth, at least 12 inches (30cm) from the main stalk to avoid impaling the tubers. Gently pry the root clump upward. Shake off loose soil, and allow the tubers to cure in a dry, frost-free garage for a few days.

Then gently pack in cardboard boxes filled with dry peat moss, vermiculite, or wood shavings. You can buy Plantonix Premium Vermiculite Bliss from Walmart to help protect dahlia and canna tubers against humidity spikes and fungal rots. Store in a dark, cool space between 40-50°F (4-10°C). Where winters are mild (zone 8 and warmer), dahlias can stay in the ground, provided soil drains freely and is protected with mulch.

Plumbing and outdoor water features carry the highest financial risk when a hard freeze arrives. Water expands by about 9% when it turns to ice. Even if your home features modern "frost-free" outdoor hose spigots, leaving a garden hose attached prevents the internal valve from draining properly. The pipe inside the wall can split, unnoticed until it leaks in spring. So uncouple every hose, stretch them out on a gentle incline to drain residual water, and roll them up for indoor storage.

If your home has an interior shutoff valve for exterior spigots, close it now. Open the outside tap to bleed the line, and then secure an insulated foam faucet cover over the fixture. You can get Frost King FC1 Foam Faucet Covers from Walmart in double packs. Drip lines can usually be drained by opening the end caps. In-ground lawn sprinkler systems require compressed air to blow all standing water out of the pipes, manifold valves, and pop-up heads. Too much air pressure can crack pipes and heads, so if you don't know your system, winterizing sprinklers is better left to a pro.

Anything holding standing water is at risk. Drain cast concrete, ceramic, and stone water fountains. Cover them with waterproof tarps to prevent pooled water from freezing and cracking the masonry. For bird baths, stone bowls will split under hard freezes, so store decorative basins indoors. To support backyard birds in winter, add a designated heated bird bath such as the Gesail Heated Bird Bath from Amazon. You can attach this to a decking or balcony and it’s a great depth for smaller songbirds. Alternatively, float a clean rubber ball or rubber duck on the surface of frost-safe metal basins to keep surface water moving.

Container-grown plants are far more vulnerable to cold than specimens planted in garden beds. Cold air surrounds the container on all sides, dropping potting soil temperatures up to 20°F lower than garden beds. An outdoor shrub rated hardy to your zone might still freeze and die if left unprotected in a pot. Roots in a pot sit above ground, with cold reaching them from the sides as well as the top. They're also less cold-hardy than the stems above them.

Tropical plants, potted citrus, pelargoniums, and tender patio perennials should be moved indoors once nighttime temperatures hover around 50°F (10°C). Before anything goes inside, inspect leaf undersides and stem junctions for hitchhiking pests like aphids, mealybugs, and spider mites, as well as webs or anything sticky. Wash the foliage with a gentle stream of water. Use Bloem Plant Caddies from The Home Depot to roll heavy container citrus plants and tropicals indoors. Keep them apart from houseplants until you're sure nothing hitched a ride.

Pots left out need plants rated a zone or two hardier than your area. For hardy perennials, boxwoods, and dwarf conifers in large patio containers, group pots along a sheltered, south-facing foundation wall. Elevate on pot feet to ensure drainage holes remain ice-free, and wrap in burlap or bubble wrap to protect root balls from freezing solid. An unheated garage is fine for many hardy potted plants: just water them now and then so the soil doesn't dry out completely.

Fall is an ideal time for planting perennials and shrubs, but specimens tucked into borders within the last 6-8 weeks have not had long to root and establish. As freeze-thaw cycles strike, frost heave can push shallow perennial crowns and strawberry plants out of the ground. Mulch can help, but you need to time this right. Wait until after the first hard freeze has cooled the ground.

Once the top inch of soil is cold, apply a 3-4-inch (8-10cm) layer of coarse, loose organic mulch to new plantings and strawberry patches. Good options are shredded pine bark, clean wheat straw, or chopped leaves. You can buy Timberline Pine Bark Nuggets from The Home Depot. Keep it pulled back 2-3 inches (5-8cm) from plant crowns to prevent moisture buildup and winter rot.

Vulnerable shrubs require above-ground shielding. Broadleaf evergreens like boxwood, rhododendron, and cherry laurel continue to lose moisture through their leaves in winter. Combined with winter sun and wind, this can cause foliage burn. Water deeply before soil freezes. Then drive stakes into the ground around vulnerable shrubs while the soil is still workable, and fasten some burlap to form a windbreak. On old-wood bigleaf hydrangeas, use these screens to protect fragile flower buds.

Porous containers left out in freezing conditions are a common source of costly winter loss. Unglazed terracotta, ceramic pots, and composite clay planters absorb water during fall rains. When temperatures plunge below 28°F (-2°C), the moisture trapped within the clay walls expands, causing pottery to crack and split. This won’t always be obvious until spring, when a planter crumbles as soon as it is lifted.

Before freezing weather arrives, empty all unused potting soil, as a damp potting mix retains water that accelerates cracking. Scrub the inside walls with a stiff brush to remove mineral salts. For a good set of stiff bristles, buy the OXO Good Grips Heavy Duty Scrub Brush from Amazon. Allow the pottery to dry in the sun, then store the containers upside down in an unheated shed, garage, or dry basement. If planters are too heavy to move inside, empty the soil, turn them upside down on wooden blocks off the ground, and secure a waterproof tarp over them.

Take a few minutes to drain all watering cans, sprayers, and outdoor hoses, storing them where residual water cannot freeze and split their seams. Finish by scraping dried soil off spades, trowels, and hoes. Wipe the blades with a rag dipped in mineral or vegetable oil. This will prevent rust while tools sit idle over the winter months.

Some common fall chores can wait until well after the first hard freeze. In fact, tackling certain jobs too early can do more harm than good. Hold off pruning deciduous shade trees, fruit trees, and summer-blooming shrubs like panicle hydrangeas until late winter or early spring. Pruning now opens fresh wounds that cannot form protective callus tissue before a severe cold sets in, increasing the risk of injury and infection.

General border clean-up and leaf raking can also be paced out. Leaving dried flower heads, ornamental grasses, and hollow perennial stems standing in early winter provides structural interest in snowy beds. More importantly, these standing seed heads supply winter food for foraging birds like goldfinches, while hollow stems provide shelter for overwintering native bees and beneficial insects.

Finally, mulching hybrid tea roses and established perennial crowns can wait until several hard freezes have pushed plants into deep dormancy and chilled the soil. Rushing to mulch warm soil traps ground heat, delaying dormancy and encouraging tender shoots. Focus your immediate energy on plumbing, tender crops, and cold-vulnerable tubers; the rest can be tidied over the coming weekends.

Need more ideas for getting the most from your plants, indoors and out, and looking for the best seasonal expert advice delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for the free Gardening Know How Newsletter!

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

I will finally have to make peace with Camera Control, and I’m not happy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Whispers in the Appleverse suggest that the iPhone's Action Button could soon be a thing of the past, set to merge with Camera Control. As the tolerant yet unenthused owner of an iPhone 16 (the first line of models to include this feature), I must confess that the news has disturbed me. I can live without Cam Control, but the Action Button? She will be missed.

The iPhone 16 burst onto the scene with the promise that it was the latest and greatest iPhone for photography, and I lapped it right up. 2 years later, I have a confession. The Camera Control button and I rarely make contact, and when we do, we often fall out. My beloved Action Button could never.

I'll admit I was swept up in the initial glamour of the Camera Control button. Watching an Apple Genius effortlessly demo the tech had me fully indoctrinated. 'I will use that scroll feature every day', I naively thought. How efficient. How innovative.

Nowadays I tend to avoid the Camera Control button, having learned my lesson trying to use it at gigs. (Pro tip: balancing a drink under your chin while desperately trying to zoom using Cam Control can lead to major beer spillage if handled incorrectly). At times, I'll use it to make taking snaps a little easier, but on the whole, I scarcely employ the help of its various touch-sensitive capabilities.

But according to prolific leaker Instant Digital, I may have to get over my distaste for Cam Control, as future iPhone models will reportedly do away with the Action Button. Typically used to control the phone's silent mode, the Action Button can be set to an array of functions such as Focus, Voice Memo, Flashlight and more. Reports suggest this feature will soon be merged with Camera Control, supposedly streamlining these quick actions under one touch-sensitive roof.

Whether it's the simple functionality of the Action Button or the tactile haptic feedback of its gentle buzz, I find myself reaching for it far more than Cam Control. While I'll be sad to see it go, I'm intrigued to see how Apple will continue to evolve its iPhone models. It feels like the loss of the Home Button all over again, but I have mourned that and moved on. I'm sure I can do the same for the Action Button.

For more Apple news, check out our MacBook Pro PROLED hands-on review or take a look at the best Mac for content creators.
