Tesla Hasn’t Had A Successful New Product Launch Since JB Straubel Stepped Down As CTO

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

As has been widely reported, Tesla is about to end Cybertruck production. The Cybertruck has been a shocking failure. I’m sure there are more detailed analyses out there somewhere, but this short response on X from Ben McCombe that Tina Casey included in her article on the matter sums it up very well:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

That’s pretty wild. A net project loss of around $2 billion. Now, I have to admit that I haven’t fact checked the numbers here, but what we know for certain is that Tesla planned to produce a lot of Cybertrucks per year and ended up producing a small fraction of that target. In fact, the annual sales target for the Cybertruck was higher than 125,000 — it was 250,000, with a goal of getting up to 500,000!

The Cybertruck was hyped up as having more reservations than any vehicle in history and was supposedly going to shatter records and transform the market. I warned people on many occasions that the reservation total was not a real reservation total and was definitely not a reliable indicator of demand because a) a reservation only required a $100 refundable deposit, and b) a ton of people made reservations after Elon Musk said that you could lock in the price of lifetime Full Self Driving when making a reservation (so, you’d only have to pay $6,000 for FSD no matter how much the cost of it went up in the following years). Anyway, the Cybertruck had other issues as well when it finally arrived. It cost about twice as much as it was supposed to cost and it had about half as much range as it was supposed to have. In other words, it was not even close to the vehicle it was supposed to be — its range per dollar cost was about ¼ what it was supposed to be.

The Cybertruck was supposed to be the next big thing after the Tesla Model Y. It was supposed to disrupt the gigantic US pickup truck market. The Model S was a superb and widely acclaimed car, if a bit more expensive than the mass market could afford. The Model S did in fact compete superbly well with the leaders in its segment, the BMW 7 Series and Mercedes S-Class. The Tesla Model X brought a similar offering in a larger package with some fun extra elements (falcon-wing doors, self-presenting doors, a third row). The Tesla Model 3 then completely shook the automotive world as a tremendous market phenomenon and perhaps the biggest product launch in history (in terms of total revenue). The Tesla Model Y was basically the Model 3 in a more popular crossover package and eventually became a much higher selling vehicle. In fact, it became the highest selling vehicle model of any kind in multiple years. Then JB Straubel stepped down as CTO.

Due to their EV passions, JB Straubel and Elon Musk were introduced to each other and had lunch together to talk about electric airplanes (Elon’s dream) and then electric cars (because JB talked more sense into Elon). Then the two of them were introduced to Marc Tarpenning and Martin Eberhard, who had founded Tesla Motors and just barely got started on the idea. After a while, Tarpenning and Eberhard left the company, and Musk and Straubel truly led its development and growth into an automotive superpower (as briefly described above). Since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, however, if one thinks about it, it’s hard to identify any successful product launch from the company or much significant improvement at all.

Straubel left his CTO position in July 2019. The Model Y was unveiled in March 2019 and was about ⅔ the same as the Model 3. It would seem that getting the Model Y designed and prepared for launch was the last big project Straubel worked on. (Note that the Semi was unveiled in November 2017 but was long delayed due to battery supply, according to Elon Musk.) Since the Model Y, what significant product launch have we seen from Tesla? There was the Cybertruck, as we’ve already discussed. That was unveiled in November 2019. When you think about the timing now, did Straubel disagree strongly with Musk on the Cybertruck and decide it was time to move on when Musk pushed forward with his idea for an electric truck? It took another 4+ years for Tesla to start delivering the Cybertruck, in contrast to Model Y deliveries starting in January 2020, less than a year after the model was unveiled. More recently, the Cybercab has been developed, unveiled, and is now in production. A consumer car smaller and cheaper than the Model 3 was never developed and unveiled — just a stripped down, decontented Model 3 — and there basically haven’t been any other product unveilings since Straubel left his role as CTO. Elon Musk has published a couple of somewhat scattered, rambling “master plans,” but we haven’t gotten any significant new products aside from the Cybertruck and the just arriving Cybercab.

Even looking over on the energy storage side of things, the Tesla Megapack was launched in July 2019, the month Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO. Did Straubel just decide he had done enough once he had finished the Megapack launch and gotten the Model Y essentially ready for market? Did he have conflicts with Musk on where to take the company from there?

Yes, there’s been incremental product development and refreshed versions of Tesla cars and energy storage systems. There are still plenty of engineers working on improving things. However, there doesn’t seem to be the same leadership in new product development, and there also isn’t the same practical, moderating element that Straubel seemingly provided to Musk’s more wild, grandiose, sci-fi inspired dreams and ideas.

Perhaps this is all misunderstanding the roles of the two men to some extent. Perhaps things would not be much different if Straubel had stayed in the CTO role and had the same influence he had before leaving. He is still on the board of directors. (What that actually means is unclear, though.) Perhaps Tesla’s success and business would still be primarily based on the Model 3 and Model Y, without new transformative products. Or perhaps we would have had a more normal pickup truck from Tesla, an electric van, better batteries, a better market rollout of the Semi, and other products. Perhaps the Cybertruck, an epic product failure, never would have been shown to the product and hyped up unrealistically with specs and figures disconnected from what was going to be possible. Perhaps we’d have a Model C (affordable, compact Tesla) rather than a Cybercab. Perhaps Tesla would be a very different company.

Who knows? We don’t know the ins and outs of JB Straubel and Elon Musk’s relationship, or how much Straubel actually led Tesla in his CTO role. We don’t know how much Straubel tampered down Musk’s worst instincts or guided product development. We still really don’t even know why Straubel left. According to Tesla, it was so that he could focus more time on Redwood Materials, but why wasn’t that business within the umbrella of Tesla in the first place? Vertical integration has long been a key element of the company. Who knows? There’s a lot we don’t know and never will. However, what we do know is that since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, we haven’t seen a significant, successful new product launch at the company. We’ll have to wait a bit longer to see if the Cybercab is one, and whether that sets a new, positive course for the future of the company or not.

sweeping concrete cutouts shape ZAO’s sculptural art center in rizhao

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

eye-shaped openings carve through the concrete structure to create an open-air cultural space beside a wooded hillside and lake.

ZAO / Zhang Ke Architecture Office has completed the Rizhao Community Art Center in China‘s Shandong province, where a sculptural concrete structure rises from a wooded hillside beside a small lake. The building appears to hover above the landscape, its heavy upper volume supported at three corners while sweeping, eye-shaped openings carve through the level beneath. From a distance, its curved surfaces seem to extend the contours of the surrounding terrain.

Located in Bailuwan, also known as Egret Bay, the project forms part of a larger residential and resort development along China’s Yellow Sea coast. Conceived as a cultural destination for the surrounding community, the center brings exhibition spaces together with areas for public gatherings and performances. Its unusual arrangement places the galleries underground, leaves the central level open to the landscape, and introduces a series of courtyards above.

A network of deep, curving concrete beams supports the upper level, transferring its weight through three structural cores that also accommodate the stairways. This allows the architects to leave much of the ground floor open, with broad openings extending toward the grassy slopes. The concrete ceiling rises and dips overhead, creating an expansive sheltered space that changes in height as visitors move through it.

Footbridges connect this central level with the surrounding roads and pathways, while its northeastern side opens toward the lake. Designed to accommodate outdoor cinema, theater performances, and community events, the space can also be used simply as a place to pass through or gather. With access from several directions, the building becomes part of the landscape’s circulation, offering a public space beneath its substantial concrete roof.

The building’s exterior is formed from cast-in-place concrete, with insulated double-layer walls that retain the grain of their timber formwork. The horizontal impressions lend texture to the large curved surfaces, particularly along the underside of the structure. At the junction between the upper and lower volumes, a narrow opening allows glimpses of the surrounding greenery to enter the otherwise enclosed concrete stairwells.

Moving upstairs, visitors encounter a very different spatial experience. Here, the structural framework defines a sequence of trapezoidal courtyards and smaller community rooms, arranged along winding passages. Inspired by the inward-facing spaces of traditional Chinese gardens, the upper level alternates between enclosed interiors and pockets of open sky. An accessible rooftop terrace eventually opens the building back toward the surrounding forest and lake.

Beneath the grassy hillside, the center’s exhibition spaces are reached by a gently sloping concrete ramp. The gallery level follows the existing topography, with a sculptural ceiling that echoes the changing contours of the ground above. Structural elements divide the interior into interconnected spaces, allowing visitors to move between more enclosed rooms and wider areas for displaying art.

Across these different levels, ZAO uses the same concrete structure to create distinctly different encounters with the landscape. The upper courtyards offer sheltered spaces for reflection, while the central floor remains exposed to the surrounding terrain. Below, the galleries turn visitors’ attention toward art within the hillside itself. Together, these spaces give the community a building that can be explored as readily as the landscape around it.

sweeping openings connect the sheltered ground floor with the surrounding landscapea network of curved concrete beams supports the elevated level

architect: ZAO (ZhangKe Architecture Office) / standardarchitecture

principal architect: Zhang Ke location: Rizhao, Shandong Province, China completion: 2024 area: 2,525 square meters client: Bailuwan Group Design structural, MEP consultant: China Architecture Design Research Group Co. photography: © Finn Chiu | @finnzhf

Broncos and Bo Nix have won 16 straight one-score games

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The Broncos keep finding ways to win games — especially close games.

Denver has won 16 straight games decided by one score or less. Quarterback Bo Nix has been the starter in each of the games.

Via NBC Sports research, the streak ties the 2023-24 Chiefs (with starting quarterback Patrick Mahomes) for the longest streak since 1950.

The Broncos last lost a one-score game in Week 3 of the 2025 season, 23-20 at the Chargers. Since then, the Broncos have won every one-score game in which they have played.

This year, they’re doing it despite being routinely outgained. For the season, they have a yardage differential of minus-658 — third worst in the NFL. And the Broncos have won three games in 2026 despite being outgained by 100 or more yards.

The record for any season is four wins, accomplished by eight different teams. With two more this year, the Broncos will have the record to themselves.

The next opportunity to win a one-score game comes on Sunday, at the Panthers in a rematch of Super Bowl 50.

How to Harness Your Existential Wanderlust

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. “Man* is by Nature a migratory animal,” the elderly Frederick Douglass reflected in an 1887 speech about his global travels. “It does not appear that he was intended to dwell forever in any one locality. He is a born traveler.”

A generation after him, Maya Angelou observed that “you only are free when you realize you belong no place — you belong every place — no place at all.”

Partway in time between Douglass and Angelou, the painter, printmaker, and philosopher Rockwell Kent (June 21, 1882–March 13, 1971) captured this vital interplay between freedom and belonging, between nature and human nature, in the preface to Wilderness (public library) — the exquisite record of the seven months he spent on a remote Alaskan island with his young son in the gloaming hour of the Spanish flu pandemic and the First World War, at the dawn of his artistic life.

We each have a particular style of wanderlust, pulled by particular types of nature that best speak to our own — those places where we most freely lose ourselves and, in consequence, find ourselves. For some, they are the austere open space of blue skies and red canyons. For me, they are the mossy old-growth forests of New Zealand and the American Pacific Northwest. For Kent, it was the severe majesty of the Great North:

It has always been hard for me to understand myself, to know why I work and love and live. Yet it is fortunate that such matters find a way of caring for themselves. I came to Alaska because I love the North. I crave snow-topped mountains, dreary wastes, and the cruel Northern sea with its hard horizons at the edge of the world where infinite space begins. Here skies are clearer and deeper and, for the greater wonders they reveal, a thousand times more eloquent of the eternal mystery than those of softer lands.

In a new preface to his journals, penned in the final year of his life, Kent looks back on the wanderlust of his youth — the roaming restlessness that had shaped his spirit, the spirit from which his art sprang, the art that established him as one of the most celebrated creators of his time — and exhorts the generations of wanderers to come:

Wander where you will over all the world, from every valley seeing forever new hills calling you to climb them, from every mountain top farther peaks enticing you. Always the distant land looks fairest, till you are made at last a restless wanderer never reaching home — never — until you stand one day on the last peak on the border of the interminable sea, stopped by the finality of that.

Contemplating the existential pull beneath it all — the rippling, resonant why of our wanderlust — Kent adds:

We are part and parcel of the big plan of things. We are simply instruments recording in different measure our particular portion of the infinite. And what we absorb of it makes for character, and what we give forth, for [our art].

These are salutary words to read a century later, as our own world is only just shaking off its straitjacket of two-year terror and becoming wanderable again.

Complement with Rebecca Solnit’s wanderlusting history of walking, then revisit Kent’s breathtaking reflections on wilderness, solitude, and creativity.

The Sand Barrels at Homestead-Miami Speedway Were Hit Again

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The sand barrels make an untimely appearance at Homestead-Miami, red-flagging the O’Reilly Series Championship Race.

With the Championship on the line, the NASCAR O’Reilly Auto Parts Series field was red-flagged at Homestead-Miami Speedway after a driver again hit the sand barrels at the pit-lane entrance.

Andrew Patterson lost control coming to the pits and spun into the sand barrels at entry. In trying to avoid hitting the barrels, Patterson kept on the gas but still clipped them with his rear right while fishtailing through the spin.

With sand everywhere, a long red-flag period followed as five drivers remain in the mix to become the 2026 O’Reilly Series Champion.

Two of those hopefuls, Carson Kvapil and Sam Mayer, entered the pits just before Anderson, after a battle for the lead.

This isn't the first time this has dramatically affected the end of a NASCAR race; in 2023, Kyle Larson crashed out of NASCAR’s Fall Homestead-Miami race while chasing what could’ve been his second championship. He was already safely locked into the Championship Four after a win the week before, but the points loss drastically hurt his chances of winning this year. It's also a mistake he can not outrun on the internet.

The reminder of this DNF comes a day before Larson needs just nine points to clinch his third championship.

Victoria Beaver is a nomadic sports writer who spends her time hopping between race tracks and hippie farms. She’s covered every corner of motorsports that will let her in from 410 Sprints to NASCAR to Supercross. Her daily driver is a 2010 Subaru that she refused to do the smallest amount of preventative maintenance on. Instead, she spends her free time and money building a 42-foot Skoolie to one day travel the country full time.

APOD: 2026 November 8 – Major Fireball Meteor

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: The sky glows with soft pinkish colors of fading twilight in this serendipitous mountaintop vista. Taken in subfreezing temperatures, the thoughtfully composed photo shows snowy, rugged peaks seen from a mountain pass on 2017 November 14. Below lies the village of La Villa, Alta Badia in Italy's Dolomite Alps. Above the nestled village lights, the constellation Ursa Major hangs over the northern horizon. But most stunning is the intense fireball meteor. It was captured during the camera's exposure by chance as it flashed east to west across the northern horizon, under Ursa Major's familiar Big Dipper asterism. Sightings of this major fireball meteor were widely reported in European skies, making it one of the most reported fireball events ever for planet Earth's American Meteor Society and the International Meteor Organization. The meteor's measured track over Germany is consistent with its origin near the active radiant of November's Northern Taurid Meteor Shower, a shower associated with dust from Encke's comet.Tomorrow's picture: witch sky?

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.
